                                               RE: Notice of Violation
                                                   Construction without a Permit

Dear Mr. Bracken:

This letter serves as a Notice of Violation of the Georgia Rules for Air Quality Control. The alleged
violations are based on information received on June 25, 2026, and a follow-up site visit on June 29, 2026,
regarding construction occurring at the Facility prior to receiving an Air Quality Permit.

Georgia Rule 391-3-1-.03(1)(a) requires that any person prior to beginning the construction or
modification of any facility, which may result in air pollution, shall obtain a permit for the construction
or modification of such facility from the Director. Information provided to the Division on June 25, 2026,
and on June 29, 2026, indicated that construction had begun at the Facility prior to receiving the permit
applied for in Permit Application #29947, submitted November 14, 2025. The Division alleges the
Company violated Georgia Rule 391-3-1-.03(1)(a).

Upon receiving this letter, the Company must stop all remaining construction of air pollution emissions
sources and/or control equipment until such time that an air quality permit is issued for the construction
and operation of the Facility. Any further evidence of the Facility continuing construction before the
Permit is issued could result in further enforcement action, including monetary penalties, if warranted.

Within 15 business days of receipt of this letter, the Company shall submit to the Division the following
information:

   1. The reason(s) why the Company commenced construction of the emission units prior to receiving
      an air quality permit to construct and operate.
   2. The date of the initial construction on any air pollution emission sources.
   3. Any other information the Company considers relevant to the alleged violation.


                                                                                        AIRS No. 217-00080

 1.          INTRODUCTION
 1.1         Project Overview
             VoltaGrid, LLC (VoltaGrid) is proposing to construct a power generation facility with a
             maximum power output of 90 megawatts (MW) consisting of 33 Jenbacher J620 natural gas-
             fired, four-stroke lean-burn, reciprocating internal combustion engines (RICE) (“the Facility”
             or “ATL-1”). The Facility is a baseload facility; that is, it is proposed to operate continuously
             for 8,760 hours per year.

             In addition to the 33 J620 RICE units, the Facility will also have two (2) 4 MMBtu/hr natural
             gas-fired fuel gas heaters and one (1) natural gas-fired black start emergency engine
             generator.

             The proposed Facility will be classified as a synthetic minor source of air emissions with
             respect to the Title V and New Source Review (NSR) permitting programs,

1.2          Source Segregation
             The subject of this permit application is the ATL-1 power generation Facility. The Facility is
             proposed to provide prime power to itself and to a nearby facility owned and operated by an
             external third party (Serverfarm facility). Construction of the ATL-1 Facility would fulfill an
             immediate need for alternative, rapidly deployable prime power to the separately owned and
             operated nearby third-party facility. As discussed in the following paragraphs, the VoltaGrid
             ATL-1 Facility is a separate stationary source from the Serverfarm facility.

             Source determinations are largely based on the regulatory definition of a “source”, legal
             interpretations of the definition, and past determinations or precedents. According to 40 CFR
             70.2 and 71.2, the definition of "major source" in Title V regulations and the definitions of
             “stationary source" and “building, structure, facility or installation" in NSR regulations [40
             CFR 52.21(b)(5) & (6), 51.165(a)(1)(i) & (ii) and 51.166(b)(5) & (6)] form the basis of the
             regulatory definition of a source.

             The source definitions establish three general conditions, ALL of which must be met for two
             or more facilities to be aggregated as a single stationary source for air permitting purposes:

                 e        All pollutant-emitting activities at such sources belong to the same industrial
                          grouping

                 e        Sources are located on one or more contiguous or adjacent properties

                 e        Sources under control of the same     person or persons under common         control

             These are discussed in detail below:

                     1.     Same industrial grouping: The primary economic activity of an emissions unit
                            determines its Standard    Industrial   Classification (SIC) code.   If the SIC major
                            group codes are different, then the pollutant emitting activities (or parts of) are
                            considered separate stationary sources. Data centers typically fall under SIC
                            Code 7374 (Data Processing & Hosting Services). Data processing is an IT
                            service, which is unrelated to energy production. Power plants typically fall under
                            SIC Code 4911 (Electric Services).

                            United States Environmental Protection Agency (USEPA) guidance suggests that if
                            a unit is considered a “support facility” for other activities, then that will affect the
                            assignment of the SIC code [45 FR 52695]. Establishments primarily engaged in

Page   | 1

                                                                                            SIP Permit Application
                                                                                                     VoltaGrid LLC
                                                                                                    Covington, GA


                          performing supporting services for other establishments of the same company
                          rather than for the general public or for other business firms” are “classified
                          industrially on the basis of the primary activity of the operating establishment(s)
                          they serve.”

                   2.     Contiguous or Adjacent: The 2019 Idsal Memo? interprets adjacent to mean
                          physical proximity for all sources except SIC 13 (oil and natural gas production).
                          In USEPA’s opinion, “functional interrelatedness of operations is not relevant.
                          While there is no bright line, USEPA notes that adjacency requires the operations
                          to be in physical proximity and should meet the “common sense notion of a plant.”
                          The proposed VoltaGrid Facility and the Serverfarm facility will be located on
                          adjacent parcels of land, but each will be fenced in order to prevent unauthorized
                          access.

                   3.     Common Control: Common control is noted as being under the control of the same
                          person or persons under common control. USEPA’‘s interpretation of common
                          control was established in the April 2018 Meadowbrook Memo? which requires
                          “power or authority to dictate the outcome of decisions of another entity, not the
                          ability to merely influence.” That is, the entity being influenced, affected, or
                          somewhat constrained by contractual relationships that it negotiated at arm’s
                          length, or by external market forces, does not necessarily mean that one entity is
                          actually controlled or governed by these influences. “Control” in this instance is in
                          terms of applicability of, or compliance with, air permitting requirements.

                        The power plant source (Facility) will be owned and operated by VoltaGrid, LLC and
                        responsibility for compliance with the power plant’s air permit lies solely with
                        VoltaGrid. The Facility will generate electric power for use to the adjacent Serverfarm
                        facility via a behind-the-meter arrangement. The Serverfarm facility’s will be owned
                        and operated by Serverfarm and responsibility for compliance with its air permit lies
                        solely with Serverfarm. Neither entity will have any control over the day-to-day
                        operations of the other, including compliance with the requirements and conditions of
                        their respective air permits.

             For the reasons discussed above, the Serverfarm facility and the VoltaGrid power plant are
             considered two separate stationary sources and do not need to be aggregated for air
             permitting purposes. This air permit application is for the VoltaGrid ATL-1 power generation
             Facility.

1.3          Purpose of the Permit Application
             Construction of a new source or modification to an existing source requires an air permit
             from the Georgia Environmental Protection Division (GA EPD). Ramboll Americas Engineering
             Solutions, Inc. (Ramboll), on behalf of VoltaGrid, has prepared this application to obtain a
             Georgia State Implementation Plan (SIP) Construction & Operating Permit under Georgia
             Rules for Air Quality Control (GRAQC) 391-3-1-.03(1) & (2). VoltaGrid will serve as the ATL-
             1 Facility owner, operator, and permit holder.


1 https://www.epa.gov/sites/default/files/2019-12/documents/adjacent_guidance.pdf
2 https://www.epa.gov/sites/default/files/2018-05/documents/meadowbrook_2018.pdf

              PROCESS           DESCRIPTION

2.1           Facility General Information
              The VoltaGrid ATL-1 Facility will be constructed adjacent to the Serverfarm facility on
              Hazelbrand Road NE in Covington, Georgia. Figure 1 shows the Facility’s location.

              The operations of the Facility are categorized under North American Industrial Classification
              System (NAICS) 221112: Fossil Fuel Electric Power Generation, SIC Code 4911: Electric
              Services.


2.2           J620   RICE    Units
2.2.1         Emission      Units
              The proposed ATL-1 Facility will consist of 33 Jenbacher J620 natural gas-fired four-stroke
              lean-burn RICE units. Each RICE unit will consist of combustion exhaust treatment, and
              equipment cooling systems will be stacked above each enclosure. Emissions from each RICE
              will be released from a 2.5-foot inside-diameter vertical stack at approximately 62 feet
              above grade.

              The mechanical power generated by the RICE units will connect to an on-site generator. The
              electrical power generated by the generator will be connected to a substation for distribution
              to the Serverfarm facility. A portion of the power generated by the RICE units will be used to
              power the ancillary, on-site equipment through a switch gear.

              A detailed site layout of the Facility, as well as a simplified process flow diagram, can be
              found in Figure 2 and Figure 3, respectively.

2.2.2         Air Pollution Control      Information
              Each RICE will be equipped with a built-in combustion exhaust treatment system, consisting
              of a selective catalytic reduction (SCR) system and oxidation catalyst. For SCR control,
              VoltaGrid proposes to use a 32.5% urea solution. The urea solution is proposed to be stored
              in bulk tanks at the Facility. The urea solution is injected into the combustion exhaust gas
              prior to the SCR bed, to aid in the reduction of nitrogen oxide (NOx) in the stack. The
              oxidation catalyst will control emissions of carbon monoxide (CO) and volatile organic
              compounds (VOC), and gaseous organic toxic air pollutants (TAP).

              Details on emission factors and facility-wide potential-to-emit (PTE) calculations are provided
              in Appendix B.

2.3           Two    (2) Natural Gas-Fired Fuel Gas Heaters
              The Facility will have two (2) heaters of 4 MMBtu/hour each. The fuel gas heaters are
              required to warm up the incoming pipeline natural gas to prevent freezing of the gas
              regulating valves under certain gas operating conditions and to avoid condensation       and
              formation of moisture in the inlet gas lines. The fuel gas heaters are proposed to be
              operated for 8760 hours/year.

              Emissions from each of the two fuel gas heaters will be released from a 1.17-foot diameter
              vertical stack approximately 13.5 feet above grade.


Page    | 3

                                                                                            SIP Permit Application
                                                                                                     VoltaGrid LLC
                                                                                                    Covington, GA


 2.4         One (1) Natural Gas-Fired Black Start Emergency                   Engine
             The Facility will also include one (1) natural gas-fired black start engine. The engine is
             required to provide emergency power to start the auxiliary systems to power the first 3620
             engine post a black out event. The emergency engine will be permitted to operate for 100
             hours/year.

             Emissions from the emergency engines will be released from a 2.33-foot diameter vertical
             stack at approximately 18 feet above grade.

 2.5         Five (5) Temporary          Natural Gas-Fired Engines
             The Facility will also include a total of five (5), trailer-mounted, Caterpillar G3520 natural
             gas-fired engines during the months of April and May 2026. Each engine is about 2.5 MW in
             power output.


             Two (2) engines are needed in April 2026 to provide for the construction-related power
             needs (approximately 1 MW required) for Serverfarm’s data halls. Three (3) engines will be
             operational   in May   2026 to provide approximately   4 MW   of power for Serverfarm’s
             construction needs.

             Pursuant to discussions with.the GA EPD, the temporary engines are being included within
             the requested synthetic minor SIP Permit even though they will be operational only for two
             months and will not overlap in operations with the main 3620 power generation units.


Page   | 4

                                                                                                   VoltaGrid LLC
                                                                                                  Covington,   GA


              EMISSION           ESTIMATES

3.1           Overview
              Pollutants emitted from the proposed emission units include NOx,CO, VOC, sulfur dioxide
              (SOQz2), particulate matter (PM), including PMig and PM2.s, hazardous and toxic air pollutants
              (HAP and TAP), and greenhouse gases (GHG), which are quantified in units of carbon dioxide
              equivalents (COze).

              The methodology used to estimate the potential hourly and annual emissions from each
              source is discussed in the sections below. Detailed emissions calculations are provided in
              Appendix B and the manufacturer’s specification sheets for the units (if applicable) are
              provided in Appendix C.

3.2           3620 RICE Units - Methodology
3.2.1         Criteria Air Pollutants
              The engine-specific emission factors provided by the manufacturer for NOx, VOC
              (hydrocarbons), CO, filterable PM, and SO2 were used to estimate the emissions of those
              pollutants for the engines under different operating conditions including steady state
              operations and startup periods. During startup periods (discussed further in Section 3.2.4),
              conservatively assumed to last 5 minutes, the control efficiency of the SCR and oxidation
              catalyst was conservatively assumed to be 0%. While the calculations conservatively assume
              0% control through the duration of the startup process, the oxidation catalyst (which is a
              passive control device) will begin oxidizing organics and CO as the engine exhaust heats up
              the catalyst bed (well before the end of the 5 min startup period).

              During normal operations, the SCR has a control efficiency of 98% for NOx control, and the
              oxidation catalyst has a control efficiency of 95% for VOC and CO, based on the
              manufacturer’s specifications.

3.2.2         Greenhouse     Gas Pollutants
              The GHG emission factors provided in 40 CFR 98 Subpart C, Tables C-1 and C-2 were used
              to estimate emissions of carbon dioxide (COz), methane (CH4) and nitrous oxide (N20).
              Emissions of GHGs as COze were calculated based on the global warming potentials provided
              in 40 CFR 98, Subpart A, Table A-1.

3.2.3         Toxic Air Pollutants
              During startup operations, the TAP emissions are assumed to be uncontrolled. The TAP
              emission factors provided in AP-42, Chapter 3.2-2, were used to estimate uncontrolled
              emissions from 4-stroke lean-burn natural gas-fired RICE units, Formaldehyde and other
              organic TAPs are assumed to be 95% controlled (similar to VOC), based on the
              manufacturer's specifications.

              Additional TAP emissions will result from operation of the SCR, where   unreacted   ammonia
              will be emitted as ammonia slip. Manufacturer guarantees were used to estimate emissions
              of ammonia at the engine stack. Manufacturer-guaranteed ammonia slip level of 5 parts per
              million by volume (ppmv) will be upheld through optimal operation of the SCR.


Page    | 5

                                                                                         SIP Permit Application
                                                                                                  VoltaGrid LLC
                                                                                                 Covington, GA


3.2.4         Startup Emissions
              Based on operational experience at other sites, VoltaGrid estimates that there could be up to
              three (3) startup events for each engine every month, with each startup event lasting up to
              five (5) minutes. The thermal heat-up and the control efficiency will increase during the (up
              to) 5-minute startup event. However, the calculations conservatively assume a 0% control
              efficiency for the entire startup duration of 5 minutes.

              As such, up to one hour per month (3 events/month/engine x 5 minutes/event) and three
              (3) hours per year will be spent in startup for each engine. During startup, the catalyst-
              based control devices (SCR and oxidation catalyst) will require time to reach the operational
              temperature range needed to optimally control emissions of NOx, CO, and VOC (including
              organic TAPs). Therefore, it is conservatively assumed that 3 hours per engine per year
              results in uncontrolled emissions, and 8,757 hours per engine per year are controlled
              emissions.

3.3           Natural Gas-Fired Fuel Gas Heaters - Methodology
3.3.1         Criteria Air Pollutants
              The engine-specific emission factors provided by the manufacturer for NOx, VOC, SO2 and
              CO were used to estimate the emissions of those pollutants for the fuel gas heaters.
              Emissions of filterable PM, PMio and PM2.5 were based on emissions factors available AP-42,
              Section 1.4, Natural Gas Combustion (July 1998). As per footnote "c" to AP-42 Table 1.4-2,
              all PM is assumed to be less than 1.0 micrometer in diameter. Therefore, PM = PMig = PM2.s5
              = PM-CON + PM-FIL. Where needed, emission factors were converted using the higher
              heating value (HHV) of 1,020 Btu/scf.

3.3.2         Greenhouse     Gas   Pollutants
              The GHG emission factors provided in 40 CFR 98 Subpart C, Tables C-1 and C-2 were used
              to estimate emissions of CO2, CH4 and N2O. The emission factors were converted from
              kg/MMBtu to Ib/MMBtu. Emissions of GHGs as CO2e were calculated based on the global
              warming potentials provided in 40 CFR 98, Subpart A, Table A-1.
3.3.3         Toxic Air Pollutants
              The TAP emission factors provided in AP-42, Section 1.4, Natural Gas Combustion (July
              1998), Table 1.4-3 and 1.4-4 were used to estimate TAP emissions from natural gas-fired
              heaters/boilers.

3.4           Black Start Emergency        Engine - Methodology
3.4.1         Criteria Air Pollutants
              The engine-specific emission factors provided by the manufacturer for NOx, VOC, and CO
              were used to estimate the emissions of those pollutants for the temporary engines.
              Emissions of filterable PM, and SO2 were based on emissions factors available in AP-42 Table
              3.2-3 (Uncontrolled Emission Factors for 4-Stroke Rich-Burn Engines). Annual emissions
              were calculated based on an annual operating rate of 100 hours/year.
3.4.2         Greenhouse     Gas   Pollutants
              The GHG emission factors provided in 40 CFR 98 Subpart C, Tables C-1 and C-2 were used
              to estimate emissions of CO2, CH4 and N20. The emission factors were converted from
              kg/MMBtu to Ib/MMBtu. Emissions of GHGs as CO2e were calculated based on the global
              warming potentials provided in 40 CFR 98, Subpart A, Table A-1.

                                                                                                Covington, GA


3.4.3         Toxic Air Pollutants
              The TAP emission factors provided in AP-42, Chapter 3.2-2 were used to estimate
              uncontrolled emissions from the black start engine.

3.5           Temporary     Engines CATG3520       - Methodology
3.5.1         Criteria Air Pollutants
              The engine-specific emission factors provided by the manufacturer for NOx, CO, VOC,
              formaldehyde and CO2 were used to estimate the emissions of those pollutants for the
              temporary engines. Emissions of PM, PMig, PMz.5, and SO2 were based on emissions factors
              available in AP-42 Table 3.2-3 (Uncontrolled Emission Factors for 4-Stroke Rich-Burn
              Engines). The temporary engines will only be operational during the months of April (2
              engines) and May 2026 (3 engines) to provide bridging power for construction of the
              Serverfarm data halls. The main J620 engines are not expected to be operational at the
              same time as the temporary engines used for bridging power.

3.5.2         Greenhouse     Gas   Pollutants
              The engine-specific emission factors provided by the manufacturer for were used to estimate
              CO emissions for the temporary engines The GHG emission factors provided in 40 CFR 98
              Subpart C, Tables C-1 and C-2 were used to estimate emissions of CH4 and N20. Emissions
              of GHGs as COze were calculated based on the global warming potentials provided in 40 CFR
              98, Subpart A, Table A-1.

3.5.3         Toxic Air Pollutants
              Formaldehyde emissions are based on manufacturer provided data. The TAP emission factors
              provided in AP-42, Chapter 3.2-2, were used to estimate uncontrolled emissions from 4-
              stroke lean-burn natural gas-fired RICE units.

             REGULATORY               APPLICABILITY
             The Facility is subject to several federal and state regulations. The following subsections
             discuss applicable regulations. It should be noted that the Facility will be located in Newton
             County, which is designated as maintenance” for the 2008, 8-hour ozone standard, but is in
             attainment for all other ambient air quality standards.

4.1          Major New Source Review           (40 CFR 52.21 and GRAQC            391-3-1)
             The NSR preconstruction permitting program is an element of air quality regulations for
             maintaining attainment with the NAAQS established by the USEPA for criteria pollutants.
             Under the federal Clean Air Act (CAA) and implementing state permitting programs, NSR
             preconstruction permitting is divided into two programs, which apply on a pollutant-specific
             basis as determined by the air quality of the area. In areas that are designated by the USEPA
             as “in attainment” or “unclassifiable” regarding a NAAQS, the construction of a new major
             source is subject to the Prevention of Significant Deterioration (PSD) NSR program for the
             attainment pollutants regulated by that NAAQS. In areas that are designated
             “nonattainment” regarding a NAAQS, the construction of a new major source is subject to
             review under the Nonattainment New Source Review (NNSR) program for the specific
             nonattainment pollutants.

             VoltaGrid understands that the 90 MW power generation facility does not meet the
             definitions for any of the 28 listed categories in 40 CFR 52.21(b)(1)(i)(A) and adopted by
             reference in GRAQC 391-3-1-.02(7)(a)(2)(iii). As such, the facility will be subject to the 250
             tons per year (TPY) major source threshold (40 CFR 52.21(b)(1)(i)(B)) for PSD. That is, the
             facility will be classified as a major stationary source if the PTE of any NSR-regulated
             pollutant (other than greenhouse gases) is 250 TPY or more.

             The NSR major source thresholds in Newton County are 250 TPY for all regulated criteria
             pollutants except for ozone (O3) precursors like NOx and VOC, which have thresholds of 100
             TPY. As presented in Section 3, PTE of each regulated pollutant is well below this threshold
             so the facility will be classified as a minor source with respect to the federal NSR permitting
             program.

4.2          SIP Permitting (GRAQC         391-3-1-.03)
             Georgia requires new or modified industrial sources to obtain a SIP construction and
             operating permit. Pursuant to the requirements of GRAQC 391-3-1-.03(1)(b), this report
             provides the information to enable the GA EPD to issue the permit. Regulations also require a
             demonstration of compliance with the applicable air quality standards for TAP, which is
             typically accomplished through air dispersion modeling analysis. The modeling analysis
             methodology and results are provided in Section 5 of this report.

             Table 4-1 identifies additional equipment to be installed at the Facility, which is not subject
             to permitting requirements and provides justification for the exemption.

               :02(8))
               New Source Performance Standards (NSPS), promulgated in 40 CFR 60 and adopted by
               reference in GRAQC 391-3-1-.02(8), provide emissions standards for regulated pollutant
               emissions from new, modified, and reconstructed sources. The following sections discuss the
               NSPS that are potentially applicable to the proposed project.

4.3.1          NSPS Subpart A - General Provisions
               NSPS Subpart A provides requirements for testing, monitoring, notifications and
               recordkeeping. Any source that is subject to another subpart under 40 CFR 60 is also subject
               to Subpart A, unless stated otherwise. Since the proposed facility will be subject to an NSPS
               (discussed later in this section), the proposed project will be required to comply with
               applicable provisions of Subpart A.

               engine was manufactured on or after July 1, 2007. Ramboll has assumed the model year of
               the proposed engines will be 2008 or later, and therefore this rule is applicable to the
               proposed SI ICE that will be operated at the facility.

4.4            National Emissions Standards                for Hazardous Air Pollutants (40 CFR              61, 63,
               and GRAQC 391-3-1-.02(9))
               The   National   Emissions Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP)         promulgated     in 40
               CFR 61 and adopted by reference in GRAQC 391-3-1-.02(9), are stationary source standards
               for HAPs from specific source categories. The       proposed    project is not subject to any NESHAP
               under 40 CFR Part 61.

               NESHAPs for Source Categories, promulgated in 40 CFR 63 and adopted by reference in
               GRAQC 391-3-1-.02(9), regulate emissions of HAPs from specific source categories. NESHAP
               set the maximum available control technology (MACT) standards for major sources of HAP. A
               facility that has potential emissions exceeding 10 tpy for any individual HAP and/or emissions
               exceeding 25 tpy for the sum of all HAPs is classified as a major source of HAP emissions. A
               facility that is not   a major source of HAPs is classified    as an area source. The   proposed
               facility is an area source of HAPs. The following sections discuss the potentially applicable
               NESHAP standards to the proposed facility.

4.4.1          NESHAP       Subpart     A
               NESHAP Subpart A provides generally applicable requirements for testing, monitoring,
               notifications, and recordkeeping. Any source that is subject to another subpart under 40 CFR
               63 is also subject to Subpart A, unless stated otherwise.

4.4.2          NESHAP       Subpart Z2ZZ2Z
               NESHAP Subpart ZZZZ applies to new and existing stationary RICE located at both major
               and area sources of HAP emissions. Per 40 CFR 63.6590(c), for new or reconstructed
               stationary RICE located at an area source of HAP emissions, the only requirement under
               NESHAP Subpart ZZZZ is to meet the requirements of NSPS Subpart IIII for compression
               ignition (CI) ICE and of NSPS Subpart JJ] for spark ignition (SI) ICE. Since the proposed SI
               ICE at the facility will be in compliance with NSPS Subpart JJJJ, the units will also be in
               compliance with NESHAP Subpart ZZZZ. No further requirements apply for these engines
               under this regulation.

4.4.3          NESHAP       Subpart DDDDD
               NESHAP Subpart DDDDD establishes national emission limitations and work practice
               standards for HAP emitted from industrial, commercial, and institutional boilers and process
               heaters located at major sources of HAP. However, the ATL-1 Facility is not a major source

               of HAP, and as such NESHAP Subpart DDDDD                  is not applicable to the proposed fuel gas
               heaters.                                                                                                          :


4.4.4          NESHAP         Subpart 333333
               NESHAP Subpart JJJJ3] applies to industrial, commercial, or institutional boilers as defined in
               40 CFR 63.11237 that is located at, or is part of, an area source of HAP. The ATL-1 Facility is
               an area source of HAP and the two 4 MMBtu/hr natural gas-fired fuel gas heaters meet the
               definition of a boiler located at an industrial facility. However, pursuant to 40 CFR
               63.11195(e), a gas-fired boiler? as defined in 40 CFR 63.11237 is exempt from the
               requirements of NESHAP Subpart J333JJ. As such, NESHAP Subpart JJJJJJ is not applicable.

4.5            Compliance Assurance Monitoring                       (40 CFR 64)
               Pursuant to 40 CFR 64.2(a), the provisions of 40 CFR 64 (CAM) are applicable to emission
               units that:

                   1.     Are subject to an emission limitation or standard unless the standard is an NSPS or
                          NESHAP   standard    promulgated       after November       15, 1990;

                   2.     Use a contro] device to achieve compliance with that emission Jimitation or standard;
                          and

                   3.     Whose pre-control emissions of the regulated air pollutant are equal to or greater
                          than 100% of the amount required for the source to be classified as a major source.

               Also, per 40 CFR 64.2(b){vi), a unit equipped with a continuous emissions monitoring system
               (CEMS) is not required to develop a CAM plan. The Facility’s PTE is not expected to be
               greater than the amount required for the source to be classified as a major source under the
               Title V program. Therefore, CAM regulations are not applicable.

4.6            Risk Management            Program        (RMP)      (40 CFR 68)
               Title III of the 1990 CAA Amendments              contains requirements for subject facilities that store
               and/or process certain hazardous substances for ensuring their safe use.                      Under these
               requirements, facilities must identify and assess their hazards and carry out certain activities
               designed to reduce the likelihood and severity of accidental chemical releases. Section 112(r)
               of the CAA, codified in 40 CFR 68, mandates the EPA to publish rules to develop and
               implement RMPs for sources with more than the threshold quantity of a listed regulated
               substance to identify, prevent, and minimize the consequences of accidental releases. The
               three elements that should be incorporated into an RMP include:
                        °     Hazard Assessment;
                          °     Prevention    Program;     and
                         e     Emergency Response Program.
               The facility proposes to use 32.5% urea reagent in the SCRs on the engines, and as such the
               RMP requirements do not apply. Additionally, there are no RMP subject substances
               anticipated to be stored onsite in quantities greater than the threshold quantities listed in 40
               CFR 68.130, Table 1. Therefore, the RMP will not apply.

4.7           Title V Program       (40 CFR 70 and GRAQC              391-3-1-.03(10))
              A Title V operating permit is required for operating the facility and is in addition to a pre-
              construction permit for any “major source” as defined in 40 CFR 70.2. To operate under a
              permit application shield, a complete and timely initial Title V permit application needs to be
              submitted within 12 months of beginning operation of the facility.

              The proposed 90 MW Facility will not qualify as a major stationary source because the
              Facility-wide PTEs are well below the applicable thresholds for the regulated air pollutants.

4.8           Acid Rain Program         (40 CFR 72 and GRAQC             391-3-1-.13))
              The Acid Rain Program is codified in Title IV of the Clean Air Act. The purpose of this
              regulation is to reduce and/or limit emissions of NOx and SOx, which contribute to acid rain.
              This regulation establishes certain general provisions and the operating permit program
              requirements for affected sources and units under the Acid Rain Program, pursuant to Title
              IV of the CAA. Further requirements for affected sources under Part 72 are outlined in 40
              CFR 73, 74, 75, 76, 77, and 78.

              Pursuant to 40 CFR 72.6(b)(9) and 40 CFR 72.7(a), new units (ones that have not previously
              lost an exemption) that serve one or more generators with total nameplate capacity of 25
              MWe or less, that burn gaseous fuel with an annual average sulfur content of 0.05 % or less
              by weight, and that do not burn any coal or coal-derived fuels are exempt from the
              requirements of the Acid Rain Program. The proposed engines will burn only natural gas and
              will not serve generators with a total nameplate capacity of 25 MWe or more. As such, the
              provisions of the Acid Rain Program are not expected to be applicable.

4.9           Greenhouse       Gas Reporting      (40 CFR 98)
              40 CFR 984 establishes mandatory      GHG   reporting   requirements for owners    and   operators of
              certain facilities that directly emit GHG as well as for certain suppliers. Per 40 CFR
              98.2(a)(1), “electricity generation units that report CO2 mass emissions year-round through
              40 CFR 75 (subpart D)” are part of the source categories presented in Table A-3 of 50 CFR
              98, Subpart A. As such, pursuant to 40 CFR 98.2(a)(3), the power plant will be required to
              report under Part 98, if the aggregate maximum rated heat input capacity of the stationary
              fuel combustion units at the facility is 30 MMBtu/hr or greater, and if the facility emits
              25,000 metric tons COze or more per year in combined emissions from all stationary fuel
              combustion sources (both conditions are expected to be satisfied). As such, GHG reporting
              obligations under 40 CFR 98 are expected to be applicable to the proposed Facility.

4.10          Georgia Rules for Air Quality Control
              In addition to the federal regulations, the GRAQC       establishes regulations applicable at the
              emission   unit level. The state regulations also include general    requirements for facilities,
              such as the requirement to obtain construction and operating permits. Source specific
              standards in the GRAQC that are potentially applicable to the facility are discussed in the
              following sections.


4 The USEPA is proposing to amend the Greenhouse Gas Reporting Program (GHGRP) to remove program
obligations for most source categories. The comment period closed on November 3, 2025,

4.10.1    GRAQC          391-3-1-.02(2)(a)           — General    Provisions
              Rule (a) broadly prohibits the emission of air contaminants which unreasonably interfere with
              the enjoyment of life or use of property. To that end, GRAQC 391-3-1-0.2(2)(a)3(ii) specifies
              that the Director can provide more specific restrictions and limitations to safeguard the
              public health, safety, and welfare of the people of the State of Georgia.

              As anew source, the Facility is required to conduct a Toxic Impact Assessment (TIA) if the
              maximum uncontrolled emissions of TAP are greater than the Minimum Emission Rate (MER)
              values identified in Appendix A of the Guideline. Each listed TAP has an established MER, a
              short-term Acceptable Ambient Concentration (AAC), and a long-term AAC. If the TAP
              emission rate from a source is above its MER level, further determination of compliance with
              the AACs   is required.   Compliance    with this requirement   and the ambient air quality
              assessment are addressed in Section 5.

4.10.2    GRAQC          391-3-1-.02(2)(b)           — Visible Emissions
              Rule (b) limits visible emissions from all sources to no more than 40% opacity, unless a
              source has another opacity limitation elsewhere in the Georgia rules. This limitation is
              applicable to the proposed emission units to be located at the facility, and the facility will
              comply with this limitation through the exclusive use of natural gas in its generator engines,
              which will result in negligible visible emissions.

4.10.3    GRAQC          391-3-1-.02(2)(d)           — Fuel-Burning Equipment
              Rule (d) provides standards for PM emissions and visible emissions from fuel-burning
              equipment. Fuel-burning equipment is defined as “equipment the primary purchase of which
              is the production of thermal energy from the combustion of any fuel.”5 The proposed
              generators do not meet this definition of fuel-burning equipment, therefore, are not subject
              to the limitations in Rule (d).

4.10.4    GRAQC 391-3-1-.02(2)(e)                    — Particulate Emission from Manufacturing
          Processes
              Rule (e), also known as the process weight rate (PWR) rule, limits emissions from
              manufacturing    processes   based     on their process input weight rates. This rule is not
              applicable to the proposed emission units as they are not manufacturing equipment.

4.10.5    GRAQC          391-3-1-.02(2)(g)           — Sulfur Dioxide
              This regulation provides emissions standards for new and existing fuel-burning sources (not
              "fuel-burning equipment"). For fuel-burning sources with a heat input capacity of less than
              100 million British thermal units per hour (MMBtu/hr), Rule (g) limits the sulfur content of
              the fuels burned to less than 2.5 % by weight (wt%). This rule is applicable to the proposed
              generators to be located at the facility. VoltaGrid will comply with this limitation through the
              exclusive use of natural gas in its engines.

4.10.6    GRAQC          391-3-1-.02(2)(n)           — Fugitive    Dust
              Rule (n) requires facilities to take reasonable precautions to Jimit fugitive dust from
              becoming airborne. Further, opacity from fugitive dust sources, such as roads and the
              handling of dusty materials, is limited to 20% opacity. VoltaGrid will comply with the


5 GRAQC 391-3-1-.01(cc)

Page   | 16

                                                                                                 SIP Permit Application
                                                                                                          VoltaGrid LLC
                                                                                                         Covington,   GA


              requirements of this rule, including during periods of construction, through appropriate
              mitigative measures.

4.10.7    GRAQC         391-3-1-.02(2)(tt) — VOC              Emissions from Major Sources
              Rule (tt) requires a review of reasonably available control technology (RACT) from sources in
              certain Atlanta metropolitan    area counties that have potential VOC       emissions exceeding     100
              TPY.® The facility will have less potential VOC emissions than this threshold, so a case-by-
              case RACT analysis is not required.

4.10.8    GRAQC        391-3-1-.02(2)(yy)           — Emissions of NOx from Major Sources
              Rule (yy) requires a case-by-case NOx RACT review for facilities located in certain Atlanta
              metropolitan area counties that have potential NOx emissions exceeding 100 TPY.’ The
              potential NOx emissions from the facility do not exceed 100 TPY. Additionally, per GRAQC
              391-3-1-.02(2)(yy)5, the requirements of this rule do not apply to individual equipment at
              the source which are subject to (jjjj), (lll), (mmm), or (nnn). As discussed in Section
              4.10.10, the proposed generators will be subject to Rule (mmm); therefore, as the
              proposed generators will be the only source of NOx at the facility, the provisions of Rule (yy)
              will not apply. Even though a RACT review is not required, since the proposed generators will
              be Tier 2 certified, they are expected to meet RACT.

4.10.9    GRAQC        391-3-1-.02(2)
                                   (lil) - NOx Emissions from Fuel-Burning                           Equipment
              Rule (ill) applies to affected fuel-burning equipment located in certain Atlanta metropolitan
              area counties that have a heat input capacity between 10 MMBtu/hr and 250 MMBtu/hr. The
              facility is located in Newton   County,    which is a listed county, and   each generator will have     a
              heat input capacity within the specified range. However, as previously discussed, the SI ICE
              do not meet the definition of fuel-burning equipment under the Georgia rules. Thus, Rule (Ill)
              does not apply.

4.10.10 GRAQC 391-3-1-.02(2)(mmm) — NOx Emissions from Stationary Gas
       Turbines and Stationary Engines used to Generate Electricity
              Rule (mmm) provides NOx emission limitations for stationary gas turbines and stationary
              engines located in certain Atlanta metropolitan area counties, including Newton County. This
              regulation is applicable to the proposed generator engines. Specifically, the emissions from
              the facility’s engines shall not exceed 80 ppm of NOx at 15% O2. Compliance with this
              emissions limitation is guaranteed by the vendor specifications.

4.10.11 GRAQC 391-3-1-.02(2)(rrr)                       - NOx Emissions from Small Fuel-Burning
        Equipment
              Rule (rrr) applies to affected fuel-burning equipment located in certain Atlanta metropolitan
              area counties that have a heat input capacity less than 10 MMBtu/hr. While the facility is
              located in the listed counties, none of the generators will meet the definition of fuel-burning
              equipment. As such, Rule (rrr) does not apply.

 5.            TOXICS        IMPACT        ASSESSMENT

The GA EPD's Guideline for Ambient Impact Assessment of Toxic Air Pollutant Emissions (“Guideline”)
provides requirements for assessing the impacts of TAP emissions from a proposed project.® The
Guideline defines a TAP as any substance which may have an adverse effect on public health,
excluding any specific substance that is covered by a state or federal ambient air quality standard. If a
facility’s emissions of a specific TAP are less than the MER, no further analysis is required for that
pollutant. Otherwise, an air dispersion modeling analysis is required to demonstrate that the facility’s
emissions of the TAP will not exceed the pollutant’s AAC. Appendix A of the Guideline provides a list of
state TAPs, as well as their respective MERs and AACs.

A comparison of the potential emissions of each TAP from the proposed facility to the MERs is provided
in Table 5-1, which indicates that a TIA is only required for seven (7) pollutants - 1,3-Butadiene,
Acetaldehyde, Acrolein, Ammonia, Benzene, Ethylene Dibromide and Formaldehyde. Each of the
seven TAPs has long-term AAC on an annual basis.

The TIA was conducted using USEPA’s SCREEN3 modeling software (version 13043), which evaluates
the maximum 1i-hour average impact. Ramboll prepared three separate model runs, one for each of
the emission units at the Facility:

       1.     One J620 engine/generator with a unit emission rate of i g/s,

       2.     One black start emergency engine with a unit emission rate of 1 g/s, and

       3.     One     fuel gas heater with a unit emission   rate of 1 g/s.


Page        | 19

                                                                                       SIP Permit Application
                                                                                                VoltaGrid LLC
                                                                                               Covington, GA


This approach conservatively models the maximum impact. The temporary engines will be onsite only
for two months and as such, were not included in the TIA.

Per Appendix A of the Guideline, the majority of the modeled pollutants have both a short-term (15-
minute average) AAC and a long-term (annual average) AAC. The proposed generators and the two
fuel gas heaters are permitted to operate continuously throughout the year, so the short-term and
long-term modeled emission rates are identical. The black start engine will be operated for 100
hours/year.

In accordance with the Guideline, the maximum facility-wide short-term 1-hour modeled impact was
converted to a 15-minute impact by multiplying by a factor of 1.32, and the maximum facility-wide
long-term 1-hour modeled impacts were converted to annual impacts by multiplying the maximum
modeled        concentration   by a factor of 0.08.

The results of the TIA are presented in Table 5-2 and demonstrate that the impacts from the
proposed Facility will be less than the AACs. The SCREEN3 model output files supporting this TIA are
provided in Attachment D.

 - Emission factors for HAPs and TAPs are based on AP-42, Section 1.4, Natural Gas Combustion (July 1998), Table 1.4-3 and 1.4-4.
4. Greenhouse gas emissions are based on 40 CFR 98, Subpart C, Tables C-1 and C-2 for natural gas combustion. The emission factors were converted from
   kg/MMBtu to Ib/MMBtu. The carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) emission factor is calculated as the sum of each GHG pollutant multiplied by its global warming

                                      GWP

 + Short-term emissions/unit (lb/hr) = emission factor (Ib/MMBtu) x heat input (MMBtu/hr)
  Short-term emissions/unit (lb/hr) = emission factor (lb/MMscf) x heat input (MMscf/hr)
 + Annual emissions/unit (tpy) = Short-term emissions/unit (ib/hr) x 8,760 hr/year x ton/ 2,000 Ib
 + Short-term emissions - total (lb/hr) = Short-term emissions/unit (lb/hr) x number of units
  Annual emissions - tatal (tpy) = Annual emissions/unit (tpy) x number of units

         Annual emissions (tpy - total) = Annual emissions (tpy per engine) * (number of engines)

30.05.2024/HT( (EDFB)                       TS JGC 620 J715 13800V VoltaGrid Vantage 23May24                                   11/58

 JENBACHER


 0.10 Technical parameters
 The following “Technical Instruction (TA) of JENBACHER’” form an integral part of the contract and must be
 strictly observed:

 TA 1000-0004 - Preservation of Jenbacher modules
 TA 1100-0110 — Boundary Conditions for Jenbacher Gas Engines
 TA 1100-0111 - General Conditions —- Operation and Maintenance
 TA 1100-0112 — Installation of Jenbacher Units

 These Technical Instructions reference other guides and instructions which can be provided upon request.
 These instructions should be carefully reviewed by all personnel involved with the application, installation
 design, installation construction, and overall maintenance of any Jenbacher gas engine

 All data in the technical specification are based on engine full load (unless stated otherwise) at specified
 temperatures as well as the methane number and subject to technical development and modifications. For
 isolated operation an output reduction may apply according to the block load diagram. Before being able to
 provide exact output numbers, a detailed site load profile needs to be provided (motor starting curves, etc.).

 All pressure indications are to be measured and read with pressure gauges (psig).

 [1] At nominal speed and standard reference conditions ICFN according to ISO 3046-1, respectively.
 [2] According to ISO 3046-1, respectively, with a tolerance of +5 %.
     Efficiency performance is based on a new unit (immediately upon commissioning).Effects of degradation
     during normal operation can be mitigated through regular service and maintenance work.

[3] Average value between oil change intervals according to maintenance schedule, without oil change
    amount
[4] At p. f. = 1.0 according to IEC 60034-1:2017    with relative tolerances, all direct driven pumps are included
[5] Total output with a tolerance of +12/-8 %
[6] According to above parameters [1] through [5]
[7] As a guiding value at p.f. 0.8 and only valid for (engine, generator, TCM). Other peripheral equipment is
    not considered.
[8] Exhaust temperature with a tolerance of +8 %
    Note: an optimized operating mode to minimize methane slip can result in changed exhaust gas data
    (exhaust gas temperature, NOx emissions, etc.) and must be taken into account in the design of the
    exhaust gas aftertreatment
[9] Mixture temperature at:
    If the engine is designed for intake air temperatures of > 34°F, then the stated mixture heat of the ist
    stage is to be increased from 25°F in 2%/°F increments. The additional temperature must be added to the
    resulting full load point.

Radio interference level
The ignition system of the gas engines complies the radio interference levels of CISPR 12 and EN 55014
class B, (30-75 MHz, 75-400 MHz, 400-1000 MHz) and (30-230 MHz, 230-1000 MHz), respectively.

 JENBACHER

 Definition of output
 e [SO-ICFN continuous rated power:
   Net break power that the engine manufacturer declares an engine is capable of delivering continuously, at
   stated speed, between the normal maintenance intervals and overhauls as required by the manufacturer.
   Power determined under the operating conditions of the manufacturer's test bench and adjusted to the
   standard reference conditions.
 e Standard reference conditions:
   Barometric pressure:                14.5 psi (1000 mbar) or 328 ft (100 m) above sea level
   Air temperature:                    77°F (25°C) or 298 K
   Relative humidity:                  30 %

 e Volume values at standard conditions (fuel gas, combustion air, exhaust gas)
   Pressure:                           1 atmosphere (1013.25 mbar)
   Temperature:                        32°F (0°C)

 Loss of engine performance
a) Performance reduction due to gas quality
If the reference methane number is not reached and the knock control responds, the ignition timing at full
performance is adjusted in conjunction with the engine management system; only then is performance
reduced.
H2 admixtures in the range of 3-5 Vol% into the natural gas network are generally regarded as non-critical.
Prerequisites for this are rates of change according to TA 1000-0300, as well as the knock resistance
(minimum methane number) of the natural gas-H2 mixture according to the specification. For reliable
compliance with required NOx emissions, the JENBACHER LEANOX?s control is recommended
(measurement of NOx emissions and correction of the LEANOX controller). Higher H2 addition rates into the
natural gas network must be assessed on a project-specific basis.

b) Performance reduction due to voltage and frequency limits
If the voltage and frequency limits for generators specified in IEC 60034-1 Zone A are exceeded, performance
is reduced.

c) Performance reduction due to environmental conditions
The ratings in the specification are valid for full load operation at a site installation of 1000 ft (300 m) and an
air intake temperature ofT1 < 104F (40C). At T1 > 104F (40C), an output deration of approximately 0.8%/F
(1.44%/C) will occur

Parameters for the operation of JENBACHER gas engines

observed: TA 1000-0004, TA 1100 0110, TA 1100-0111, and TA 1100-0112.
Transport by rail should be avoided. See TA 1000-0046 for further details

Failure to adhere to the requirements of the above-mentioned TA documents can lead to engine damage and
may result in loss of warranty coverage.

The engine is tested on the test stand with a specific natural gas. The natural gas as used by the Seller on
the test stand may differ from the gas as ultimately used by the Customer. For this reason, performance and
efficiency levels, exhaust-gas temperatures, gas consumption, etc. may deviate from the parameters _
specified in the contract.

Ready for H2 means a possible adaptation up to 100vol% H2 operation. Performance data, timeline and

 0.20 Mode of Operation

 Pure Island Mode
 The genset is operating as standalone solution, or together with other power generation equipment,
 connected to a load without any connection to the utility. The unit(s) can perform "Black-out" start without
 external auxiliary power supply to the "dead busbar”. A single unit is controlled by the dedicated genset
 controller DIA.NE XT4, for multiple units a higher-level control system needs to be provided either by
 JENBACHER or the customer to synchronize the gensets among each other.

 The load adding and shedding capabilities of the genset documented in
 e   TA 2108-0031 - general island operation
 e TA     2108-0027 for type 2 engines
 e TA     2108-0025 for type 3 engines
 e TA     2108-0029 for type 4 engines
 e TA     2108-0026 for type 6 engines
 e TA     2108-0032 for type 9 engines
 needs    to be considered by the customer in order to ensure proper operation of the equipment.

Generator - voltage range: +/-10% of generator Un

 0.30.20 Possible mains operator requests

To protect the generating unit in mains parallel operation, appropriate mains protection monitoring functions
are necessary to disconnect the generator from the mains in case of a mains fault.

The mains operator-dependent specifications such as e.g.: voltage and frequency range, active power
limitation, load ramps, reactive power limitation and control, protection concept, necessary certification or
declarations, process data and interfaces are to be specified in project enquiries and must be agreed with
JENBACHER before conclusion of the contract.

e    Selectivity assessment, protection tests and recurring tests: on-site by the system operator
e    Control power provision via pool operator: on request e.g. primary, secondary, tertiary
e    Black start capability and countering in own use: on request
e    Power generation system (EZA) controller or central control: on-site or possible on request
e    Process data scope / remote control:
     e System data must be provided by the connectee for the mains operator.
     e Remote control interface to the mains operator: on-site
     e Interface specification!

Billing measurements - installation, operation, maintenance and remoie data transmission: on-site.

Models of genset and generator: simplified models executed as effective value models for mains parallel
operation optionally available.
Model formats: Powerfactory, or PSS/E (as of PP23)
Validated genset models in Powerfactory according to FGW TR3, TR4 and TR8 by a body accredited for this
purpose according to DIN EN ISO/IEC 17065

e static voltage stability
e dynamic mains support

 0.30.20.01 Active power adjustment in the event of overfrequency and
 underfrequency

 The following functions are available:
 e LFSM-U: Limited Frequency Sensitive Mode - Underfrequency
 e LFSM-O: Limited Frequency Sensitive Mode - Overfrequency
 e   FSM

 Reduced power output at overfrequency: (LFSM-O function)
 The frequency threshold is freely adjustable from fn + (200 - 500mHz) and the static from 2% to 12%.
 Unless the relevant mains operator specifies otherwise for the LFSM-O mode, a threshold of fn + 200mHz and
 a static of 5% is set.

Power increase in the event of underfrequency (LFSM-U function) — (OPTIONAL as of XT4.5)
activated according to the mains operator's specifications
The frequency-sensitive active power feed-in has the effect that the generating plant
also moves permanently up and down on the frequency characteristic curve ("driving on the characteristic
curve") in the frequency range between fn - 200mHz (unless otherwise specified by the mains) and fn - 2.5Hz
with regard to its maximum possible active power feed-in.
The prerequisite for this is a corresponding power setpoint.

Reduced power output at underfrequency:
below 98% of fn, reduction by standard 10% of maximum capacity per Hz. Reduction up to maximum fn - 6%.
Lower reduction ramps of 2 - 10%/Hz on request
The FSM function is available as an option

The power generation system is capable of continuing to operate at this minimum power when the minimum
power for controllable operation is reached.

  JENBACHER

  1.00 Scope of supply - Module
 Design:
 The module is built as a compact package. The engine base is bolted to the gearbox/generator base.
 The Engine output shafting is connected through a coupling to a gearbox. A second coupling is then
 provided connecting the gearbox to the generator. To provide the best possible isolation from the
 transmission of vibrations, the engine rests on the engine base-frame by means of anti-vibration mounts. The
 remaining vibrations are eliminated by mounting the complete module (engine and gearbox/generator
 frames) on isolating pads (e.g. Sylomer). This, in principle, allows for placing of the module to be directly on
 any floor capable of carrying the static load.


 1.01 Spark ignited gas engine
 Four-stroke, air/gas mixture turbocharged, aftercooled, with high performance ignition system and
 electronically controlled air/gas mixture system.
 The engine is equipped with the most advanced

 LEANOX®          LEAN-BURN COMBUSTION SYSTEM

 developed by JENBACHER.

 1.01.01 Engine design
Engine block
Single-piece crankcase and cylinder block made of special casting; crank case covers for engine inspection,
welded steel oil pan.

Crankshaft and main bearings
Drop-forged, precision ground, surface hardened, balanced; main bearings (upper bearing shell: grooved
bearing / lower bearing shell: sputter bearing) arranged between crank pins, drilled oil passages for
forced-feed lubrication of connecting rods.

Vibration damper
Maintenance free viscous damper

Flywheel
With ring gear for starter motor and additionally screwed on.

Pistons
Two-part steel piston with oil passages for cooling; piston rings made of high-quality material, main
combustion chamber specially designed for lean burn operation.

Connecting rods
Drop-forged, heat-treated, big end diagonally split and toothed. Big end bearings (upper bearing shell: sputter
bearing / lower bearing shell: sputter bearing) and connecting rod bushing for piston pin.


                                                                                                        INNEO
30.05.2024/HT( (EDFB)              TS JGC 620 J7 15 13800V VoltaGrid Vantage 23May24                      20/88

  JENBACHER

  Cylinder liner
  Chromium alloy gray cast iron, wet, individually replaceable.

  Cylinder head
  Specially designed and developed for JENBACHER-lean burn engines with optimized fuel consumption and
  emissions; water cooled, made of special casting, individually replaceable; Valve seats and valve guides and
  spark plug sleeves individually replaceable; exhaust and inlet valve made of high quality material; Pre-
  chamber with check-valve.

  Crankcase breather
  Connected to combustion air intake system

 Valve train
 Camshaft, with replaceable bushings, driven by crankshaft through intermediate gears, valve lubrication by
 splash oil through rocker arms.

 Combustion air/fuel gas system
 Multipoint fuel injection by cylinder individual Port Injecten valves to allow highly accurate and dynamic
 control of fuel composition. Exhaust driven turbocharger with Wastegate, water-cooled intercooler.

 Ignition system
 Most advanced, fully electronic high-performance ignition system, external ignition control.
 MORIS / SEMIC: Automatically, cylinder selective registration and control of the current needed ignition
 voltage.

 Lubricating system
 Gear-type lube oil pump to supply all moving parts with filtered lube oil, pressure control valve, pressure
                                                                                                             relief
 valve and full-flow filter cartridges. Cooling of the lube oil is arranged by a heat exchanger.

 Engine cooling system
 Electrical jacket water pump, complete with distribution pipework and manifolds.

Exhaust system
Turbocharger and exhaust manifold

Exhaust gas temperature measuring
Thermocouple for each cylinder

Electric actuator
For electronic speed and output control

Electronic speed monitoring for speed and output control
By magnetic inductive pick up over ring gear on flywheel

Starter motor
Engine mounted electric starter motor

  1.03 Module equipment
 Module frame
 Welded steel profile frame for mounting the engine, generator and heat exchangers

 Flexible coupling
 Plug-in, backlash-free coupling with torque limiter, connecting the engine and generator. The coupling
 isolates the generator from the main harmonic vibrations of the alternating torque of the engine.

 Coupling
 With torque limiter, to connect the gearbox and generator

 Coupling housing
 For covering both couplings

 Flexible mounting
 Rubber rails spaced evenly at the centre of gravity between the engine and the frame and the generator and
 the frame respectively, and Sylomer strips between the module frame and the foundation base plate to
 insulate against vibrations

Gearbox
Single-stage spur gear box with upper drive shaft and incorporating its own lubricating oil supply, mounted on
the module frame. The lubricating oil heat exchanger is integrated in the hot water circuit. The transmission
ratio is 1:1.2.

Exhaust gas piping on the module
Connection to the turbocharger, including a compensator for taking up the thermal expansion and for isolation
against vibrations

Intake air filter
Dry air filter with replaceable filter cartridges, flexible connections to the gas mixer, maintenance indicator for
filter inspection.

Exhaust system flushing:
The exhaust system is purged after a false start; this is achieved either with an exhaust gas purge fan or by
turning the engine with the gas valves closed

Interface cabinet
Fully enclosed sheet steel upright cabinet, door with rubber sealing strip. Mounted on the module, wired and
ready for operation.

Paintwork: RAL 7035

Degree of protection: IP54 outside, IP20 inside (protection against accidental direct contact with live
components)

Designed to comply with EN 61439-2 / IEC 61439-2
Ambient temperature 5 - 40°C, 70% relative humidity.

                                                    bance     ne                 Bo    Lem   neater   antennal”        Baging Signal


The SCR Oxi-Kat system is comprised of the following elements

Pre-Oxidation Converter:
e Located directly after the engine exhaust
e Contains 2 layers of oxidation catalyst consisting of a metal carrier body (OXI) or a metal frame and
  ceramic support structure (ASC) with precious metal coating to reduce CO and CH2O emissions.
« The catalyst elements are supplied separately and installed in the Pre-Oxidation Converter housing after
  the engine has been commissioned and adjusted via a service door.

Urea Mixing Section:
e Located directly after the Pre-Oxidation Converter
« Contains urea injection dosing nozzles and mixing structures to promote efficient mixing of the exhaust
  gases with the injected 32.5% urea solution.

SCR converter:
e Located immediately downstream of the mixing section
e Houses 3 layers of SCR catalyst to convert the nitrogen oxides in the exhaust stream into environmentally
  neutral nitrogen in a catalytic converter with the aid of the reducing agent urea.
e Ammonia Slip Control elements are located within the SCR converter.
e The SCR converter will also provide a exhaust noise silencing feature

Set-up:
e SCR catalytic converter housing for outdoor installation
e the urea metering panel is intended for installation close to the injection point
  (supplied within the container)

1.11.01 Remote messaging over MODBUS-TCP

Data transfer from the JENBACHER module control system to the customer's on-site central control system
via MODBUS TCP using the ETHERNET 10 BASE-T/100BASE-TX protocol TCP/IP.

The JENBACHER module control system operates as a SLAVE unit.
The data transfer via the customer's MASTER must be carried out in cycles.

Data transmitted:
Fault messages, operating messages, measured values (generator power, oil pressure, oil temperature,
cooling water pressure, cooling water temperature, etc.) according to JENBACHER standard (interface list).

JENBACHER          limit of supply:
RJ45 socket at the interface module in the module               control cabinet


1.11.06 Remote Data-Transfer with DIA.NE XT4

General
DIA.NE XT4 offers remote communication using an Ethernet connection.

1.) DIA.NE XT4 HMI
DIA.NE XT4 HMI is the Human-Machine-Interface of DIA.NE XT4 engine control and visualization system for
JENBACHER gas engines.
The system offers extensive facilities for commissioning, monitoring, servicing and analysis of the site.
By installation of the DIA.NE XT4 HMI client program it can be used to establish connection to site, if
connected to a network and access rights are provided.
The system runs on Microsoft Windows Operating systems (Windows 7, Windows 8, Windows 10)


                                                                                                    INNES
30.05.2024/HT( (EDFB)                   TS JGC 620 J715 13800V VoltaGrid Vantage 23May24              38/58

 JENBACHER

 Function
 Functions of the visualization system at the engine control panel can be used remotely. These functions
 provide control, monitoring, trend indications, alarm management, parameter management, and access to
 long term data recording. By providing access to multiple systems, also with multiple clients in parallel,
 additional useful functions are available like
 e   Multi-user system
 e   Remote control
 e   Print and export functions
e    Data backup.
The DIA.NE XT4 is available in several languages.

 Remote Operation:
Option1 - remote request/remote blocking (remote start/stop)
lf the operating mode selector switch on the module control cabinet is set to "Automatic" and the request
mode selector is set to "Remote", the module can be enabled (requested) or blocked (derequested) by a
control element (button) on the DIA.NE XT4 HMI client. The request can come from a Windows PC in the
local network or over a secure myPlant remote connection. The myPlant remote connection requires the
myPlant Care package as a prerequisite (not included in the price)
Note:
This option also allows an additional on-site request (from the hardware or a data bus) or self-managed
operation (JENBACHER station control, mains import control, etc.) to be implemented.

Option2 — Remote Acknowledgement (remote reset}
Error messages can also be acknowledged remotely on the DIA.NE XT4 HMI client, apart from those error
messages incorporated in the safety loop - see TA 1100-0111, Section 12, for more information. Remote
acknowledgement can come from a Windows PC in the local network or over a secure myPlant remote
connection. A myPlant remote connection - myPlant care package is required to use the "remote reset" over
the Internet (not included in the price). Use of this function requires an agreement to be concluded between
the customer and JENBACHER laying down the procedure to be followed in the event of damage caused by
a remote acknowledgement (preserving evidence) and how to establish responsibility. Proof of OPT training
(operator training) and TJE (training on the job) is also required.

Scope of supply
   e Software package DIA.NE XT4 HMI Client Setup (Download)
   e Number of DIA.NE XT4 HMI - Client user license (Simultaneous right to access of one user to the
     engine control)

                        Nr. of license         Access
                        1                      1 Users can be logged in at the same time with a PC
                                               (Workplace, control room or at home).
                        2 -“n“ (Optional)   | 2-“n” Users can be logged in at the same time with a PC
                                               (Workplace, control room or at home).
                                                If 2- “n" users are locally connected at Computers from office
                                               or control room, then it is not possible to log in from home.


Caution! This option includes the DIA.NE XT4 HMI client application and its license only — NO secured,
encrypted connection will be provided by JENBACHER! A secured, encrypted connection — which is
mandatory — has to be provided by the customer (via LAN connection or customer-side VPN), or can be
realized by using option myPlant™.

JENBACHER

 1.11.13 Out-of-step protection / pole slip protection (integrated in DIA.NE
XT4)
ANSI Function Code 78
e 3-phase monitoring, integrated in DIA.NE XT4 controller
e Uses voltage measurement at the generator and engine speed measurement
  (supplied by JENBACHER).
e Allows real-time calculation of rotor angle during dynamic operations
e Allows safe detection of a pole slip risk and allows operation up to the maximum limit value
e Acting on generator circuit breaker and generator de-excitation.
e Alarm message on the DIA.NE screen.
e Active in Grid and Island parallel operation

OPTIONAL on special release:
separate digital protection relay (ATTENTION: different detection as generator and mains voltage are
evaluated. Tripping only possible if pole slip has occurred)

Following monitoring are integrated in the DIA.NE generator protection package
   e Load angle / pole slip monitoring
   e Exciter failure monitoring [ANSI 40]


1.11.14 Generator Overload / Short Circuit Protection

ANSI Function Code 50/51
e Digital protection relay, 3-phase, integrated into the module control panel.
e Connected to the protective current transformers in the generator star point
e Acting on the generator circuit breaker and on the generator de-excitation
e Alarm message on the DIA.NE screen

Characteristics / settings:
e Seiting for overload: to 1,1 times of the generating set rated current,
e Dependent time characteristic acc. to IEC 60255-151: very inverse, time multiplier setting 0,6.
e Setting for short circuit: to 2,0 times of generating set rated current,
e Independent time characteristic: 300 ms (800 ms when dynamic network support).


1.11.15 Generator Differential Protection

ANSI function code 87
e Digital protection relay, 3-phase, integrated into the module control panel.
e Connected to the protective current transformers in the generator star point (JENBACHER scope of supply)
  and to the protective current transformers in the generator circuit breaker panel (current transformers by
  client, secondary 1A, optionally: 5A).
e Acting on the generator circuit breaker and on the generator de-excitation
e Alarm message identified on the DIA.NE screen

In plants with a unit generator-transformer configuration the protection is realized as generator/transformer
differential protection.

 1.11.25 Control Strategy and Options (USA)
 Unit Additional Features and Options

 1.11.34 Frequency sensitive control (FSM)
The FSM function is used to support the stabilization of the frequency around the nominal value by moving
the active power operating point when the frequency moves outside a settable dead band.
In case of over frequency, the active power frequency response is limited by the minimum regulating level.
(e.g. Pmin 50%)
In case of under frequency, the active power frequency response is limited by the maximum capacity which is
available. (e.g. Pa 100%)

The frequency thresholds and the droop settings are specified in the national Grid Codes and/or are specified
by the network operator in the connection agreement.
Possible dead band can be set between 0 and 500 mHz

Active power frequency response capability of power-generating modules in FSM:

2.12 Gas warning device

Function:
The gas warning device continuously monitors the radiated air in the engine room and warns against gases
which are injurious to persons’ health and against explosive gas concentrations.
The measuring head (catalytic sensor) is attached on the covering or nearby the ground, dependent upon the
gas source.

Scope of supply:
e Alarm unit                 voltage:                      24VDC
e 2 Gas sensor(s)


2.13 Smoke warning device
Function:
The smoke warming device in combination with the optical smoke detector (installed in the control room) and
the thermal smoke detector (installed in the engine room) provide extensive early warning signal.

Design:
The device has an optical display for alarm and operation.
The smoke warning device is installed in a plastic housing.

Scope of supply:
e Alarm unit                 voltage:                      24V
  3 Smoke detector(s)

3.03.01 Exhaust gas silencer

Material:
Stainless steel


Consisting of:
e Exhaust gas silencer
e Flanges, seals, fixings

Insulation:
The insulation for reducing surface irradiations (heat and sound) of the exhaust gas silencer is not included in
our scope of supply and must be provided locally. The insulation (4 inch (100 mm) rock wool covered with
0,03 inch (0,75 mm) galvanized steel sheet) is required to keep the sound pressure level of the container (55
dB(A) in 32 ft (10 m)).


3.03.05 Exhaust gas blow-off valve
When the risk of backfires in the exhaust gas system exists an exhaust gas blow-off valve must be installed.
The exhaust gas can be released to atmosphere in case of a backfire.
The blow-off valve should be installed as close to the engine exhaust exit as possible.

Scope:
1.piece Exhaust gas blow-off valve


3.10.03 Cooling system — dual-circuit radiator
The heat produced by the engine (jacket water, lube oil, intercooler) is dissipated through a radiator, installed
outside.

Consisting of:
e Radiator
e Pump
e Electrical control
e Expansion tank

The radiator is designed for an ambient temperature of max. 110°F.
