    JENBACHER

    Base frame and floor construction


    Container base frame:
    The base frame consists of two continuous longitudinal bars with steady transverse
    bars for the admission of switchgears, auxiliaries and other internal equipment.
    The lower side of the base frame is insulated with 1.97 inch (650 mm) sandwich panels.

    Container floor construction:
    e Engine room: 0.12 inch (3 mm) galvanized steel sheets edged to on close floor pan. The whole floor
       construction is executed as a fully isolated oil retention tray to hold back liquids from the genset.
    e Control room: 1.57 inch (40 mm) false floor tiles plates, above anti-static PU-floor covering, color grey

    Walls


    Walls engine module:
    e Walls consist of 4 inch (100 mm) thick sandwich elements.
    e These sandwich elements consist of rock-wool insulation and galvanized steel sheet inside and outside.

    Walls ventilation module:
    e Walls consist of 3.15 inch (80 mm) thick sandwich elements.
    e These sandwich elements consist of rock-wool insulation and galvanized steel sheet inside and outside.

    Roof

e     The structure of the roof-level frame is almost identical to that of the base frame
      (shorter base frame and higher distance between transverse bars).
e     The same sandwich panels as used for the walls are fitted above the transverse bars.
e     Two 1.97 inch (50 mm) thick noise insulating elements on top of each other is mounted on the roof of the
      ventilation module. On the upper side of the roof a 0.031 inch (0.8 mm) aluminum sheet metal is placed to
      ensure that the roof is waterproof.

Doors


e     Door leaf surface: galvanized steel; material thickness 0.039 inch (1 mm), composite structure cemented
      all over and absolutely level with sealing plate. Door leaf rebated.
e     Rubber sealing in the frame and in the door leaf rebate. Thickness of door leaf: 2.44 inch (62 mm).
e     Structural bonders: Ball-bearing structural bonders of stainless steel for three-dimensional adjustment.
e     Movable wing: with panic bolt function D, with anti-panic bar inside (option), door handles inside and
      outside.
e     Step grids at doors and rain sheds above doors is installed for weather protection.

External doors:
1 piece: h = 80.7 inch (2050 mm), w = 78.74 inch (2000 mm)
5 pieces: h = 80.7 inch (2050 mm), w = 39.37 inch (1000 mm)

Internal door:
1 piece: h = 80.7 inch (2050 mm), w = 31.5 inch (800 mm)

3.20.04 Ventilation System

Container ventilation — Engine Room:
The ventilation system consists of a variable frequency drive (VFD) controlled air flow system through the
whole engine room to maintain adequate airflow and to ensure the cooling of the whole engine and
auxiliaries.

Air intake filtration:
e The air intake system is equipped with class G4 filters.

Removal of radiation heat in engine room by:
e     Axial-high performance fans
      (Number of fans and type depending on engine setup and site conditions)

Control:
e Air Intake flaps operate through E-actuating drive (230V or 24V).
e Fans and flaps are controlled by DIA.NE XT controls.
e Axial-high performance fans are frequency controlled.

Container ventilation system — Control Room:
Removal of radiation heat in control room by
e 1 piece Axial-ventilator
e Air condition for control room

 3.20.05 Serviceability Systems

 Crane system:
 Every mobile power plant is equipped with a maintenance service crane located in the engine room with a
 maximum load of 1433 Ibs (650 kg).

 External ladder:
 An access ladder with fall restraint cage designed of aluminum is mounted on the outside of the containerized
 unit to ensure full service accessibility.

 Guarding rails:
 The whole unit is - where needed - enclosed with guarding rails at the ventilation module. Hot dip galvanized,
 without lacquering.

External working platform:
Where needed for service purposes, a full working platform including walkways is installed.

Lifting points:
 Four lifting points are integrated in the roof frame on every single module.
A lifting instruction is included as a standard in the technical documentation.

Shipping

Engine module:
The fully containerized genset including mounted generator (valid for J612-J620) and all interconnections to
the ventilation module is shipped as one package.

Roof Elements:
The containerized Emissions treatment system will ship, as will the separately installed enclosure air exhaust
ducting, engine exhaust stack with silencer, and finally the pre-assembled V-Cooler. All piping, cabling and
interconnections of these items will be provided.

Documentation

Quality documentation as well as operator's manual (Description, usage, spare parts overview, operating
manuals, drawings, datasheets etc.) is supplied.

5.02 Factory tests and inspections
The individual module components shall undergo the following tests and inspections:

5.02.01 Engine tests
 Carried out as combined Engine- and Module test based on ISO 3046-3 at JENBACHER test bench. The
following tests are made at 100% load, and the results are reported in a test certificate:
e Engine output
e Fuel consumption
e Jacket water temperatures
® Lube oil pressure
e Lube oil temperatures
e Boost pressure
e Exhaust gas temperatures, for each cylinder

5.02.02 Generator tests
Carried out on the premises of the generator supplier.

 5.02.03 Module tests
 The engine will be tested with natural gas (methane number 94). The performance data achieved at the test
 bench may therefore vary from the data as defined in the technical specification due to differences in fuel gas
 quality.
 Carried out as combined Engine- and Module test commonly with module control panel at JENBACHER test
 bench, based on ISO 8528-6. The following tests are made and the results are reported in a test certificate:
 Visual inspection of scope of supply per specifications.
 e Functional tests per technical specification of control system.
   e Starting in manual and automatic mode of operation
    e Power control in manual and automatic mode of operation
    e Function of all safety systems on module
 e Measurements at 100% load:
    e Frequency
   e Voltage
   e Current
   e Generator output
   e Power factor
   e Fuel consumption
   e Lube oil pressure
   e Jacket water temperature
   e Boost pressure
   e Mixture temperature
   e Exhaust emission (NOx)

The module test for operating frequenzy 50 Hz and 6,3-6,6kV / 10,5kV-11kV will be carried out with the
original generator, except if itis not possible because of the delivery date. Then a test generator will be used
for the module test.

the manufacturers’ certificate will be provided.
In the case of a container unit the above mentioned test procedure for the module is performed in Jenbach.

The following criteria apply for demonstration purposes:

        1)   Operation will be on Combustion Air and Natural Gas which           must meet the INNIO Jenbacher gas quality
             requirements stated in the Technical Instruction 1000-0300.
        2}   Emission values are based on the fuel gas analysis (Appendix |).
       3)    Based on the nominal mass flow provided by the project specific data sheets.

             Island mode).

  INN                                                                                         JENBACHER
        5)            The emission values apply after the ATS has reached the specified functional temperature, the reductant
                      dosing release was given, and the Emissions Controller has balanced all actuators to the target value.
        6)            To manage the drift in emission quality of the engine, resulting from an expected upward drift coming from
                      fuel or oil deposits build up in the engine, VoltaGrid shall regularly monitor and control emissions.
        7)            VoltaGrid shall manage this drift through regular maintenance and repair schedules along with the use of
                      genuine INNIO Jenbacher parts and components.
        8)            NOx drift can be compensated up to a certain extent, by calibration of engine operating parameters in the
                      DIA.NE XT controls system by specially trained qualified personnel.
        9)            Excessive deposits resulting from gas or oil related contamination may require the cleaning of the
                      combustion chamber, turbochargers and aftertreatment system.
        10)           Maintenance and component repairs for the INNIO Jenbacher Gas Engines and ATS equipment is carried
                      out by qualified personnel strictly according to the schedule and repair requirements set by INNIO
                      Jenbacher along with the use of genuine INNIO Jenbacher parts and components.
     11      ~—       Testing to determine compliance with this commitment will be at the expense of the customer and
                      accomplished by a certified laboratory chosen by the customer. The engine/installation is to be in good
                      working order consistent with INNIO Jenbacher recommended maintenance practices prior to any testing.
                      INNIO Jenbacher reserves the right to participate and/or challenge the results of any testing.


If the engine              fails to meet   the emissions   representations   the customer   must provide the following supporting
documentation to INNIO Jenbacher:
     1)               Fuel gas samples
     2)               Complete maintenance records
     3)           A    full report including the calculations and results of any emissions testing.


INNIO Jenbacher will be given a reasonable amount of time to take any or all of the following actions:
    e   Perform additional testing in an effort demonstrate the emissions representations. If this testing
        demonstrates compliance with no adjustments required to the engine, customer will pay for added testing.
        If testing fails to demonstrate compliance with the emissions representations, the testing will be paid for
                  by INNIO Jenbacher.
    e             Make such adjustments to the engine so as to bring the engine into compliance with the emissions limits
                  provided in this letter.


In case the must meet emission performance guarantees are not achieved after the cure period INNIO Jenbacher
will replace the affected Unit.

                                                                GAS ENGINE TECHNICAL DATA
  G3520                                                                                                                                          CATERPILLAR’
 FUEL USAGE GUIDE:
 This table shows the derate factor and full load set point timing required for a given fuel. Note that deration and set point timing adjustment may be required as the
 methane number decreases. Methane number is a scale to measure detonation characteristics of various fuels. The methane number of a fuel is determined by using
 the Caterpillar methane number calculation.


 ALTITUDE DERATION FACTORS:
 This table shows the deration required for various air inlet temperatures and altitudes. Use this information along with the fue) usage guide chart to help determine actual
 engine power for your site.

 ACTUAL ENGINE RATING:
 To determine the actual rating of the engine at site conditions, one must consider seperately, limitations due to fuel characteristics and air system limitations. The fuel
 usage guide deration establishes fuel limitations. The altitude/temperature deration factors and the RPC (reference to the Caterpillar Methane Program) establish air
 system limitations. RPC comes into play when the altitude/temperature deration is less than 1.0 (100%). Under this condition, add the two factors together. When the
 site conditions do not require an altitude/temperature derate (factor is 1.0), it is assumed the turbocharger has sufficient capability to overcome the low fuel relative
 power, and RPC is ignored.

 To determine the actual power available, take the lowest factor between 1) and 2).
 1) Fuel usage Guide Deration
 2) 1-{(1-Altitude/Temperature Deration) + (1-RPC))

 AFTERCOOLER HEAT REJECTION FACTORS(ACHRF):
To maintain a constant air intet manifold temperature, as the inlet air temperature goes up, so must the heat rejection. As altitude increases, the turbocharger must work
harder to overcome the lower atmospheric pressure. This increases the amount of heat that must be removed from the inlet air by the aftercooler. Use the aftercooler
heat rejection factor (ACHRF) to adjust for inlet air temp and altitude conditions. See notes 30 and 31 for application of this factor in calculating the heat exchanger
sizing criteria. Failure to properly account for these factors could result in detonation and cause the engine to shutdown or fail.


 INLET AND EXHAUST RESTRICTIONS FOR ALTITUDE CAPABILITY:
 The altitude derate chart is based on the maximum inlet and exhaust restrictions provided on page 1. Contact factory for restrictions over the specified values. Heavy
 Derates for higher restrictions will apply.

NOTES:
1. Generator efficiencies, power factor, and voltage are based on standard generator. [Genset Power (ekW) is calculated as: Engine Power (bkW) x Generator
Efficiency], [Genset Power (kVA) is calculated as: Engine Power (bkW) x Generator Efficiency / Power Factor]
2. Rating is with two engine driven water pumps. Tolerance is (+)3, (-)0% of full load.
3. Efficiency represents a Closed Crankcase Ventilation (CCV) system installed on the engine.
4. Genset Efficiency published in accordance with ISO 3046/1, based on a 1.0 power factor.
5. Thermal Efficiency is calculated based on energy recovery from the jacket water, lube oil, ist stage aftercooler, and exhaust to 248°F with engine operation at ISO
3046/1 Genset Efficiency, and assumes unburned fuel is converted in an oxidation catalyst.
6. Total efficiency is calculated as: Genset Efficiency + Thermal Efficiency. Tolerance is +10% of full load data.
7. 1SO 3046/1 Genset fuel consumption tolerance is (+)5, (-)0% at the specified power factor. Nominal genset and engine fuel consumption tolerance is + 2.5% of full
load data at the specified power factor.
8. Air flow value is on a ‘wet basis. Flow is a nominal value with a tolerance of +5 %.
9. inlet manifold pressure is a nominal value with a tolerance of + 5%.
10. Inlet manifold temperature is a nominal value with a tolerance of + 9°F.
11. Timing indicated is for use with the minimum fuel methane number specified. Consult the appropriate fuel usage guide for timing at other methane numbers.
12. Exhaust temperature is a nominal value with a tolerance of (+)63°F, (-)54°F.
13. Exhaust flow value is on a ‘wet basis. Flow is a nominal value with a tolerance of + 6 %.
14. Inlet and Exhaust Restrictions are maximum allowed values at the corresponding loads. Increasing restrictions beyond what is specified will result in a significant
engine derate.
15. Emissions data is at engine exhaust flange priorto any after treatment.
16. NOx values are the maximum values expected under steady state conditions.
17. CO, THC, NMHC, NMNEHC, and HCHO are the maximum values expected under steady state conditions. THC, NMHC, and NMNEHC do not include aldehydes.
An oxidation catalyst may be required to meet Federal, State or local CO or HC requirements.
18. VOCs - Volatile organic compounds as defined in US EPA 40 CFR 60, subpart JJJJ
19, Nominal values at standard lab conditions. Site specific results may vary
20. Exhaust Oxygen tolerance is + 0.5; Lambda tolerance is + 0.05.      Lambda and Exhaust Oxygen level are the result of adjusting the engine to operate at the specified
NOx level.
21. LHV rate tolerance is + 2.5%.
22. Heat rejection to jacket water value displayed includes heat to jacket water alone. Value is based on treated water. Tolerance is + 10% of full load data.
23. Heat rejection to atmosphere based on treated water. Tolerance is + 50% of full load data.
24. Lube oil heat rate based on treated water. Tolerance is + 20% of full load data.
25. Exhaust heat rate based on treated water. Tolerance is + 10% of full load data.
26. Heat rejection to exhaust (LHV to 77°F) value shown includes unburned fuel and is not intended to be used for sizing or recovery calculations.
27. Heat rejection to A/C - Stage 1 based on treated water. Tolerance is +5% of full load data.
28. Heat rejection to A/C - Stage 2 based on treated water. Tolerance is +5% of full load data.
29. Pump power includes engine driven jacket water and aftercooler water pumps. Engine brake power includes effects of pump power.
30. Total Jacket Water Circuit heat rejection is calculated as: (JW x 1.1) + (OC x 1.2) + (1AC x 1.05) + [0.73 x (1AC + 2AC) x (ACHRF - 1) x 1.05]. Heat exchanger
sizing criterion is maximum circuit heat rejection at site conditions, with applied tolerances. A cooling system safety factor may be multiplied by the total circuit heat
rejection to provide additional margin.
31. Total Second Stage Aftercooler Circuit heat rejection is calculated as: (2AC x 1.05) + [(1AC + 2AC) x 0.27 x (ACHRF - 1) x 1.05]. Heat exchanger sizing criterion is
maximum circuit heat rejection at site conditions, with applied tolerances. A cooling system safety factor may be multiplied by the total circuit heat rejection to provide
additional margin.

SOUND PARAMETER DEFINITION:
Sound Power Level Data - DM8702-03

Sound power is defined as the total sound energy emanating from a source irrespective of direction or distance. Sound power level data is presented
under two index headings:
Sound power level -- Mechanical
Sound power fevel — Exhaust

Mechanical: Sound power level data is calculated in accordance with ISO 3747. The data is recorded with the exhaust sound source isolated.

Exhaust: Sound power level data is calculated in accordance with [SO 6798 Annex A. Exhaust data is post-catalyst on gas engine
                                                                                                                               ratings labeled as
“Integrated Catalyst".

Measurements made in accordance with !SO 3747 and ISO 6798 for mechanical and exhaust sound level only. Frequency bands outside the displayed
                                                                                                                                                     ranges are
not measured, due to physical test, and environmental conditions that affect the accuracy of the measurement. No cooling system noise is included
                                                                                                                                                  unless
specifically indicated. Sound level data is indicative of noise levels recorded on one engine sample in a survey grade 3 environment.

How an engine is packaged, installed and the site acoustical environment will affect the site specific sound levels. For site specific sound level guarantees,
                                                                                                                                                               sound
data collection needs to be done on-site or under similar conditions.

          Serverfarm ATL2 Data Center

Dear Ms. Dorrough:

This letter and its attachments constitute a Synthetic Minor Source State Implementation Plan (SIP)
Air Permit Application for Serverfarm’s proposed development of the ATL2 data center campus
(facility) at 10835 Hazelbrand Road NE in Covington, Georgia.

This application is composed of the following elements to meet the requirements for permitting in the
jurisdiction of the Air Protection Branch of the Georgia Environmental Protection Division (GEPD) of
the Department of Natural Resources:

      •   Attachment 1 – Air Permit Application Forms and Supporting Information
          −   Application Forms 1.00, 2.00, 2.01, 4.00, and 7.00
          −   Vicinity Map and Site Plan
          −   Process Flow Diagram
          −   Tax Plan Record
          −   Equipment Specifications
          −   Neighboring Facility Information
      •   Attachment 2 – Emissions Data
          −   Emissions Calculation Summary Tables
          −   Microsoft Excel Spreadsheets with Emission Calculations (Electronic)
      •   Attachment 3 – Regulatory Applicability
          −   Regulatory Applicability Summary


SEATTLE
155 NE 100th Street, Ste 302, Seattle, WA 98125 T 206.631.8680                           landauinc.com

                                              Synthetic Minor Source State Implementation Plan Permit Application
                                                                                             September 26, 2025


    •   Attachment 4 – Ambient Air Impact Evaluation
        −   Summary of Air Dispersion Modeling Methods and Results
        −   Air Modeling Files (Electronic)

The ATL2 campus is located in Newton County, which is designated as an attainment area, and as
demonstrated by the emissions estimates in Attachment 2, the data center is not a major stationary
source. Therefore, this source is not subject to major source permitting requirements. This permit
application has been prepared to demonstrate compliance with all applicable state and federal air
regulations and emission standards pursuant to the Clean Air Act, especially as set forth for new
sources in attainment areas.

The remainder of this letter provides a brief narrative summarizing details of the project and contents
of each attachment.

Attachment 1. SIP Permit Application Forms and
Supporting Information
Serverfarm has proposed the development of a data center campus in Covington that will be
comprised of one building totaling 498,960 square feet. The buildings will house banks of computer
servers and will be equipped with stable electrical power delivery systems, air cooling and cleaning
systems, and emergency backup diesel power generation capability. The project will also include site
infrastructure, such as development of internal roads for traffic egress/access and internal circulation,
and parking for employees and visitors, as well as all required utility corridors to support the data
center building. A vicinity map, a site plan, and a tax plan record for the proposed development are
included in Attachment 1.

From an air quality standpoint, the equipment of interest includes 36 3.3-megawatt (MW) diesel-
powered emergency generators and one 2.5-MW diesel-powered emergency generator. Each
generator is also equipped with 12,000-gallon capacity diesel belly tanks that store ultra-low sulfur
diesel (ULSD) fuel for use by the generators. The capacity is a maximum estimate and the final tank
size may be smaller. The data center buildings also include chillers for cooling buildings. However, the
chillers are self-contained with no external refrigerant tanks and do not emit drift droplets; therefore,
they are not sources of regulated emissions. GEPD application forms, a simplified process flow
diagram, and generator specifications are provided in Attachment 1.

Based on Landau’s experience estimating emissions from similarly sized belly tanks storing diesel fuel,
emission are anticipated to be minimal (e.g., less than 1 ton per year [tpy] of volatile organic
compounds [VOCs]).

The generators will be run for the purposes of:

    •   Startup and commissioning, which is to occur once in each generator’s lifetime
    •   Routine maintenance and testing according to manufacturer recommendations


2                                                                                                 landauinc.com

                                            Synthetic Minor Source State Implementation Plan Permit Application
                                                                                           September 26, 2025


    •   Internal system failures
    •   Emergency use to provide power to the facility when the data center’s primary power source is
        interrupted.

Planned and unplanned generator usage and the resulting emissions of criteria and hazardous air
pollutants (HAPs) will be limited so as not to trigger a requirement for the facility to obtain a Title V
permit. Additionally, Serverfarm requests a permit limit of less than 500 operating hours per year for
each generator in accordance with GEPD Rule 391-3-1-.02(2)(mmm)(4)(v).

Attachment 2. Emissions Data
Serverfarm’s generators include a diesel-powered engine that drives an alternator section to produce
electricity. Overall emissions from a diesel generator are produced from the diesel engine, and no
emissions are produced by the alternator. The terms “generator” and “engine” are used
interchangeably in this report. State and federal air quality regulations apply only to the emissions
from diesel engines.

Criteria air pollutant, HAP, and greenhouse gas (GHG) emission rates were estimated for all
generators based on the technical specifications provided by the manufacturers (Attachment 1), mass
balance calculations, and published emission factors from the US Environmental Protection Agency
(EPA) AP-42, Fifth Edition, Volume I, Chapters 3.3 and 3.4 for Stationary Internal Combustion Sources
(EPA 1996a; b).

During any event for which the emergency generators will supply power to the server system, the
generators will operate at the load needed based on the electrical demand. Therefore, the pollutant-
specific maximum emission rate under any generator operating load was assumed for calculating the
worst-case emission rates.

The proposed generators will be certified by the manufacturer to meet EPA Tier 2 emission standards
for non-road diesel engines. The manufacturer-reported not-to-exceed generator emission rates were
used to estimate emissions of oxides of nitrogen (NOX), particulate matter (PM), carbon monoxide
(CO), and VOCs. All petroleum hydrocarbons are conservatively considered VOCs for the purpose of
these emission estimates. For sulfur dioxide (SO2) and HAPs with fuel-based emission factors, the
emissions from the worst-case operating load of 100 percent was assumed.

Annual GHG emissions were calculated using the emission factors in 40 Code of Federal Regulations
(CFR) Part 98. GHG emissions are reported as total carbon dioxide equivalent using the global
warming potentials in 40 CFR Part 98, Subpart A.

Serverfarm is proposing to accept a NOX emission limit of 99.9 tpy to remain below the major
source/Title V permitting threshold. Serverfarm will keep records to document operations from each
generator, including date and time of operation, operating load, duration of run, and whether runs
are for an emergency or nonemergency purpose. These records will be used to track NOX emissions
each month, and on a rolling 12-month basis to demonstrate that facility emissions are continually


3                                                                                               landauinc.com

                                             Synthetic Minor Source State Implementation Plan Permit Application
                                                                                            September 26, 2025


equal to or less than 99.9 tpy. For the purpose of NOX emission tracking, emissions for each run will be
calculated based on the manufacturer’s not-to-exceed emission rates at 10, 25, 50, 75, and 100
percent load.

NOX emission recordkeeping will be completed for all generator run scenarios, including
nonemergency purposes, which include commissioning and periodic maintenance and readiness
testing, as well as emergency use during electric utility outages.

Summary tables presenting diesel generator potential-to-emit for criteria pollutants and HAPs are
provided in Attachment 2. Additionally, not-to-exceed emissions data from the manufacturer are
provided in Attachment 1. An electronic version of the emission calculations is being provided in
Microsoft Excel format.

Attachment 3. Regulatory Applicability
Newton County is designated as in attainment with the National Ambient Air Quality Standards
(NAAQS) for criteria pollutants. Based on proposed facility-wide emissions for the facility, the facility is
not subject to the Prevention of Significant Deterioration (PSD) program as outlined in 40 CFR
§ 51.166. Similarly, based on proposed facility-wide emissions, the facility is not subject to the federal
Title V operating permit requirements outlined in 40 CFR Parts 70 and 71. Emissions from the facility
will remain below Title V thresholds of 100 tpy of CO, NOX, PM, PM10, PM2.5, SO2 and VOCs, below 25
tpy of HAPs, and below 10 tpy of any one HAP.

Emergency diesel generators are subject to requirements under the New Source Performance
Standard (NSPS) Subparts A and IIII, as well as the National Emissions Standards for Hazardous Air
Pollutants (NESHAP) Subparts A and ZZZZ. A detailed table of applicability and regulatory
requirements is provided as Attachment 3.

The facility is also subject to state requirements as detailed in Georgia Administrative Code (GAC)
391-3-1-.02.

Attachment 4. Ambient Air Impact Evaluation
As required, a Toxic Air Pollutants (TAPs) assessment was conducted for the project. The assessment
included the quantification of TAP emissions for comparison against 20 percent of the minimum
emission rates (MERs), and an air dispersion modeling assessment with AERMOD of any TAPs that
exceed 20 percent of their respective MERs (formaldehyde and benzene). The maximum ground-level
concentrations (MGLC) for formaldehyde and benzene were compared to the allowable air
concentration (AAC). Attachment 4 includes a summary of the air dispersion modeling methodology
and results.

The toxics assessment was conservatively conducted with the generators at 100 percent load because
it resulted in the greatest emissions. The annual emission rates assumed each generator would
operate the maximum allowable number of hours (500 hours/year/generator).


4                                                                                                landauinc.com

                                                                   Synthetic Minor Source State Implementation Plan Permit Application
                                                                                                                  September 26, 2025


As shown in Attachment 4, the MGLCs for formaldehyde and benzene are below their respective
AACs.

(kbaker@landauinc.com; 425-329-0305) with any questions or concerns regarding the contents of this
permit application.

HEAVY DUTY RAINCAPS


Bergari Solutions offers heavy duty carbon steel raincaps for industrial exhaust systems. These
raincaps are all welded, counterweighted designs with an integral replaceable metal bushing at the
pivot point. Painted with a high temperature black finish.

   •   Standard models are sized to fit nominal tubing sizes.
   •   Optional pipe sizes are available.
   •   Similar models are also available in 304 and 316 grade stainless steel.
   •   An aluminum thermal spray coat finish is also available as an option.

                             Separate Source Determination
As requested by GEPD during the pre-application meeting, information is being provided regarding
VoltaGrid’s proposed facility that is being located adjacent to the southwest of the Serverfarm data
center.

VoltaGrid is proposing to construct and operate an electrical generating facility. VoltaGrid will install and
operate natural gas-fueled generators to produce electricity. Serverfarm will enter into a contract with
VoltaGrid to purchase the electricity as a primary power source for its data center.

Based on GEPD’s guidance, it is understood that VoltaGrid and Serverfarm will require separate permits
for their facilities since they are considered separate “sources.” According to Part 70.2 of Title 40 of the
CFR, for two facilities to be considered the same source, all three of the following conditions must apply
the following:

    •   Located on one or more contiguous or adjacent properties
    •   Belong to the same industrial grouping defined by the Standard Industrial Classification (SIC)
        code
    •   Under control of the same person (or persons under common control).

The two facilities will be located on one contiguous property; however, they do not belong to the same
industrial grouping, nor are they under common control.

Serverfarm’s SIC code is 7374, defined as “computer processing and data preparation and processing
services.” Whereas VoltaGrid’s SIC code is 4911, defined as “electric services.”

Additionally, Serverfarm and VoltaGrid’s facilities are not considered to be under common control for
the following reasons:

    •   Serverfarm and VoltaGrid are separate companies with no cross ownership or direct control
        over each other’s operations
    •   The Serverfarm management team will not be able to in any way dictate the operation of
        VoltaGrid and vice versa
    •   Each of their respective facilities will retain full responsibility for compliance with all air
        regulatory obligations
    •   There will be no sharing of workforces, management, administrative functions, equipment, or
        pollution control responsibilities.

Based on 40 CFR Part 70.2 and according to guidance outlined in the EPA’s Meadowbrook memorandum
dated April 30, 2018, it is understood that Serverfarm’s and VoltaGrid’s facilities are considered separate
sources.

                    ATTACHMENT 44
                    ATTACHMENT

Ambient Air
Ambient Air Impact
            Impact Evaluation
                   Evaluation

                Air Dispersion Modeling Methods and Results

A4.1-1          Introduction
This section presents the methodology and results of the ambient air quality impact assessment of TAPs
and an assessment of compliance with the minimum emission rate (MER) and Acceptable Ambient
Concentrations (AACs). Copies of the electronic modeling files prepared in support of the project will be
provided to GEPD via a file transfer site. The assessment follows the methodology from GEPD Guideline
for Ambient Impact Assessment of Toxic Air Pollutants (GEPD 2017).

As discussed in the following subsections, the modeled ambient impacts expected from project
emissions are either less than the MERs or less than the AACs.

A4.1-2          Air Dispersion Modeling
Air dispersion modeling was conducted in general accordance with the EPA’s Revision to the Guideline
on Air Quality Models: Adoption of a Preferred General Purpose (Flat and Complex Terrain) Dispersion
Model and Other Revisions; Final Rule (EPA 2005). The AERMOD modeling system was used in
accordance with the EPA’s Revision to the Guideline on Air Quality Models (EPA 2005) to estimate
ambient pollutant concentrations beyond the site property boundary.

Ambient air impacts were modeled for all TAPs for which compliance was not demonstrated via
emissions threshold screening. The most recent version of AERMOD (Version v24142) was used at the
time the modeling was completed. AERMOD requires input from several pre-processors (described
below) for meteorological parameters, downwash parameters, and terrain heights. AERMOD uses data
from pre-processor programs (i.e., meteorology and terrain) as well as emission estimates and physical
exhaust release point characteristics to predict ambient concentrations attributable to the proposed
project. The model calculates concentrations based on various averaging times (e.g., 1 hour, 24 hours,
annual, etc.) for a defined network of receptors; these concentrations are used to assess compliance
with regulations that use ambient concentrations as criteria.

The AERMOD model was used to estimate the short-term (i.e., 15-minute) and long-term (i.e., annual
average) impacts of benzene and formaldehyde whose emissions exceeded their respective MERs as
discussed in “First Tier Screening of Toxic Air Pollutant Impacts.”

Building Downwash Input Parameters

Building downwash occurs when the aerodynamic turbulence in the wake of buildings or structures
causes exhaust from an elevated source (i.e., a stack) to mix with winds and be rapidly conveyed toward
the ground, resulting in elevated ground-level pollutant concentrations. The software program Building
Profile Input Program-Plume Rise Model Enhancements (BPIP PRIME) was used to determine whether
exhaust from emission units would be affected by nearby building structures. In general, a stack is
considered to be affected by a given structure if the height of the stack is less than the height defined by
the EPA’s Good Engineering Practice (GEP) stack height methodology.

                  Air Dispersion Modeling Methods and Results
GEP stack height is defined as the height of the nearby structure(s) measured from the ground-level
elevation at the base of the stack plus 1.5 times the lesser dimension, height, or projected width of the
nearby structure(s). All exhaust stacks at the facility will be less than the calculated GEP heights and,
therefore, influenced by building downwash. To account for this potential building downwash,
parameters calculated by BPIP PRIME were provided as inputs to AERMOD. A summary of buildings and
structures is provided in Table 1. Figure 1 shows the facility site plan with the labeled buildings and stack
locations.

Table 1. Building and Structure Information
                                             Length    Width    Height
 Building/Structure Description   Quantity
                                              (feet)   (feet)   (feet)
 Data Center Building                1       1,211.5   432.5      50
 Primary Generator Enclosures       36        73.3     13.9      13.1
 Admin Generator Enclosure           1        27.3     10.6      12.7

Terrain Height Input Parameters

The digital elevation model (DEM) data source used to determine terrain heigh input parameters for
AERMOD was the National Elevation Dataset (NED) in 1/3 arc-second resolution. Due to significant
differences between the available NED data and the post-construction terrain, the post-construction
onsite terrain and the NED data were merged. This was accomplished by first building a DEM grid from
the ATL2 Grading Plan dated August 15, 2025, which was provided to Landau as topographic contours in
DWG file format. These contours were used to create a terrain grid using the Geographic Information
System (GIS) software and then combined with the NED original data. Outside of the property boundary,
the NED data remains unchanged. The revised DEM grid is provided along with the electronic modeling
files and a Figure 2 includes a topographic map of the facility and surrounding area.

Receptor Grid

To include the effects of terrain on calculated ambient concentrations, AERMOD requires information
about the surrounding terrain. The American Meteorological Society (AMS)/EPA Regulatory Model
Terrain pre-processor (AERMAP, Version 24142) was used to obtain the hill height scale and the base
elevation for each receptor.

The receptor grid spacing increases with distance from the facility, as listed below:

    •    10-meter (m) spacing along the property boundary
    •    100-m spacing from the property boundary to 2,000 m
    •    200-m spacing from 2,000 m to 5,000 m.

AERMAP requires the use of topographic data to estimate surface elevations above mean sea level.
Digital topographic data, in the form of NED files, for the analysis region were obtained from the US
Geological Survey national map downloader website (USGS 2025) and processed for use in AERMOD.
The NED used for this project have a resolution of approximately 10 m (⅓ arc-second).

                   Air Dispersion Modeling Methods and Results
The implementation of AERMAP produces a receptor file (*.rec) that contains the calculated terrain
elevations and hill height scales for each receptor. A separate *.rec file produced for each receptor
spacing group was used as an input file provided to AERMOD.

Meteorological Data

Consistent with GEPD guidance, the 5-year metrological data that was used in the modeling simulation
was downloaded from the GEPD website1 on August 6, 2025. The GEPD processed meteorological files
using AERMET (v24142), AERSURFACE (v20060), and AERMINUTE (v15272). The consecutive 5 years
(2019–2023) of meteorological data were generated with 1-minute wind data for all ASOS stations. The
following data set was used:

    •    Surface Station: Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International Airport (KATL)
    •    Upper Air Station: GA/Peachtree City-Falcon Field, GA (KFFC)
    •    Processing Option: ADJ_U* (adjust surface friction velocity for low wind/stable conditions).

The KATL station was selected because it was the Automated Surface Observing Systems (ASOS) station
assigned to Newton County.

Rural vs Urban Classification

The selection of rural or urban dispersion coefficients followed the land use classification procedure
outlined in Section 7.2.1.1.b of the Guideline on Air Quality Models 40 CFR 51, Appendix W (EPA 2024)
and the example of rural versus urban determination provided by Kim Byeong with GEPD on August 8,
2025.

Categories 23 and 24 of National Land Cover Database (NLCD) 2021 are considered as “urban.” The
percentage of urban NLCD pixels for urban areas is 13.46%, which means 86.54% of the area is rural and
the rural classification was used. See “ATL2.aersurface.log” provided within the electronic modeling files
for a detailed breakdown of the NLCD percentages.

Emission Unit Release Parameters

Modeled sources for this analysis consisted of 37 emergency generators. Release parameters used for
the modeling are summarized in Table 2. The release parameters correspond to generator operation at
100 percent, which results in the greatest emission rate. The stack parameters were provided by the
generator vendor and are included in Attachment 2. Note the final stack height and diameter may vary
from what is provided; however, these represent the minimum and maximum height and diameter,
respectively.


1 Georgia AERMET Meteorological Data | Environmental Protection Division

                 Air Dispersion Modeling Methods and Results
The generators exhaust will have a rain cap installed (see vendor specification in Attachment 2). At low
velocities, the rain cap can partially obstruct the exhaust flow, while at high velocities (such as at 100
percent load), the rain cap opens fully and the stack is unobstructed. To account for the variation in
obstruction at various loads, this assessment conservatively assumed the generators’ exhausts were
POINTCAP sources.

Table 2. Release Parameters
                                                                    Stack
                                    Emissions    Stack Height     Diameter     Temperature    Exit Velocity
         Generator Type             Source ID        (m)             (m)           (K)            (m/s)
2.25 MW Baudouin
                                      A01            5.08            0.6          784.15         32.19
Admin/House Generator

3.3 MW Baudouin 16M55G8D2/6         G01-G36          5.34            0.6          733.15         43.89

Abbreviations and Acronyms:
   K = Kelvin
   m/s = meters per second
   g/s = grams per second

First-Tier Screening of Toxic Air Pollutant Impacts

A first-tier TAP assessment includes a comparison of expected maximum emission rates with the MER.
For each TAP with an emission rate that exceeds the MER, a comparison of predicted maximum ambient
concentrations with the AAC is performed. Table 3 shows the facility-wide emission rates for each TAP
expected to be released by the facility and compares each emission rate with the corresponding MER.
For this assessment, because the generator stacks are conservatively assumed to be a capped source,
comparison to 20 percent of the MER is required. As shown in Attachment 2, maximum facility-wide
emissions of formaldehyde and benzene are expected to be greater than their corresponding MERs, so
an ambient impact analysis was completed for those TAPs.

The modeled emission rates for formaldehyde and benzene are included in Attachment 2. The annual
emission rate in grams per second was calculated assuming 500 hours/year/generator of operation and
then divided by 8,760 hours per year to determine the annual average rate.

Predicted Toxic Air Pollutant Ambient Concentrations

The first-tier ambient concentration screening analyses are summarized in Table 3. These screening
analyses were conducted for TAPs with expected maximum emission rates that exceed 20 percent of the
applicable MERs (see Table 2). As shown in Table 3, all maximum ground level concentrations (MGLC)
are less than the applicable AACs.

       Firm   Contract       Quantity:     Finn Base Load quantities of gas will be delivered based on the
       Customer' s average daily quantities as requested by Customer. SELLER will make commercially
       reasonable efforts to supply such requested Finn Base Load quantities, including entering into firm
       wholesale contract obligations necessary to supply such requested Firm Base Load quantities
         Contract Obligations").       Customer will have a right to review the Contract Obligations before

       gas through the      meter   and will be scheduled   based   on Exhibit"   C". Volumes   above the Firm Base

       Load quantities per day will be considered interruptible volumes and are subject to curtailment.


                  Special Provision: During peak, operational flow order days, CUSTOMER will limit its
                  daily gas takes from the SELLER to those hourly volumes as shown in Exhibit"                 C".    Any
                  volumes requested above those volumes shown in Exhibit" C" will be considered
                  interruptible volumes and subject to availability.

II.    Interruptible     Contract     Quantity:    The SELLER will provide Interruptible Natural gas Service
       for all timely nominated and confirmed quantities of up to 9, 600 MMBtu per day in excess of the
       Finn Base Load Quantity stated in section I, subject to confirmation on the availability and the
       curtailment provisions contracted in the Natural Gas Sales Agreement.

III.   Monthly Charges for Firm Contract Quantity: The sales price of gas shall be comprised ofthe
       following components for all firm and interruptible gas and Exhibit" B" shows the sample rate:

A.     Firm Contract        Quantity Commodity Price:           For firm base load quantities, the rate is defined as
       NYMEX LD delivered to the City Gate of SELLER, plus any Contract Obligations entered into on
       behalf of CUSTOMER and not covered by such rate.

B.     Interruptible Commodity Price: For interruptible quantities, the rate is defined as the daily
       market price of natural gas based on timely notices. Customer may request to purchase Interruptible
       Quantities in advance of the month, daily with timely notice, and daily without timely notice.
       Timely notice is 8: 00 a.m. ET five business days prior to the start of the month, 8: 00 ET on business
       day ahead of gas flow and ratable volumes over weekend/holidays, or not less than two hours prior
       to ID2 pipeline nomination deadline.

C.     Pipeline    Fuel Cost:       The rate for fuel retention shall reflect the effective     cost of TRANSCO'            s
       percentage of fuel retained as allowed for in their tariff.

D.     Pipeline Transportation Charge: The rate of TRANSCO' s firm transportation reservation fee
       applicable to the firm quantities per day, and the applicable commodity transportation charges, plus
       all applicable surcharges on the actual volumes delivered, as identified in TRANSCO' s tariff. Total
       firm monthly volumes shall be determined as those firm contract quantities times the number of
       days in the month;       the   balance of usage shall be considered "       interruptible"   volumes.         For all

NARRATIVE

TO:       Cynthia Dorrough
FROM:     Alexander Lagunas
DATE:     December 2, 2025

     Facility Name:        VoltaGrid ATL-1 EGU
         AIRS No.:         217-00080
           Location:       Covington, GA (Newton County)
      Application #:       29947
Date of Application:       November 12, 2025


Background Information

VoltaGrid LLC proposes the construction and operation of VoltaGrid ATL-1 EGU, a greenfield power
generation facility with a maximum power output of 90 MW, consisting of 33 natural gas-fired, four-stroke
lean-burn (4SLB) reciprocating internal combustion engines (RICE), two (2) 4 MMBtu/hr natural gas-fired
fuel gas heaters, and a natural gas-fired black start emergency generator. The facility will be a baseload
facility, operating continuously for 8,760 hours per year. The facility proposes to operate up to five 3,628
hp natural gas-fired temporary engines for two months during the construction of the facility.

The facility will provide prime power to itself and to a nearby Serverfarm facility (AIRS: N/A), owned and
operated by an external third party. Based on EPA guidance, the two facilities are considered separate
sources because they do not meet the three general conditions: same industrial grouping, contiguous or
adjacent, and under common control. The two facilities are not in the same industrial grouping because
VoltaGrid ATL-1 EGU is a power plant and the nearby Serverfarm facility is a data center. The two facilities
are on adjacent parcels of land but will be fenced off to prevent unauthorized access. The two facilities are
not under common control based on the April 2018 Meadowbrook Memo. As a result, the two facilities are
considered separate sources.

The mechanical power generated by the RICE units will connect to an on-site generator. The electrical
power generated by the generator will be connected to a substation for distribution to the Serverfarm facility.
A portion of the power generated by the RICE units will be used to power the ancillary, on-site equipment
through a switch gear. The fuel gas heaters will warm up incoming pipeline natural gas to prevent freezing
and avoid condensation and formation of moisture. The black start emergency engine will provide power to
the ancillary systems to power up the first RICE unit post a black out event.

The RICE units are equipped with a built-in combustion exhaust treatment system, consisting of a selective
catalytic reduction (SCR) system and an oxidation catalyst.

The facility proposes a limit of 99.0 tpy of carbon monoxide (CO) emissions in order remain under the
major source threshold with respect to Title V. The facility also proposes a limit of 0.022 lb formaldehyde

SIP Application Review                                                     VoltaGrid ATL-1 EGU, Application No.29947

per hour per engine. This limit has demonstrated that formaldehyde will remain below its allowable ambient
concentration (AAC) based on SCREEN3 modeling, thus requiring no further refined modeling.

The facility is a currently unpermitted greenfield project and located in Newton County, which is in
attainment for all criteria pollutants.

Purpose of Application

VoltaGrid LLC submitted App. 29947 for the construction and operation of VoltaGrid ATL-1 EGU, a
greenfield power generation facility, which consists of 33 natural gas-fired 4SLB combustion engines, two
natural gas-fired fuel gas heaters, and one natural gas-fired black start emergency generator. The facility
will be a synthetic minor with respect to Title V by limiting CO emissions to 99.0 tpy. The facility will have
an emission rate limit for formaldehyde. The Public Advisory period ended on December 19, 2025, and
comments were received by the Division.

Emissions Summary

Based on 8,760 hours of operation of all emission units, except for 100 hours of operation for the black
start engine, the facility would be a major source with respect to Title V. The Permittee proposes a CO
emission limit of 99.0 tpy to ensure facility-wide CO emissions remain below the major source threshold.
Since CO is the limiting pollutant, limiting CO below the major source threshold for Title V, all other
criteria pollutants will be under the major source threshold as a result. The Permittee proposes to operate a
SCR and oxidation catalyst system at all times the engines are in operation to control CO emissions, making
the facility a synthetic minor source with respect to Title V.

CO emissions will be tracked using the hours of operation of each emission unit and an associated emission
factor, in lb/hr. The generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33) have two operating modes: startup and
steady state. Startup consists of approximately five minutes in which the control devices are not operating
at expected efficiency. The facility estimates three (3) startup events for each generator per month. Steady
state is the normal operation of the generators and control devices. Each operating mode will have its own
emission factor. For the purposes of calculating emissions, startup will consist of five minutes at startup
and 55 minutes at steady state for a combined one hour. CO emission factors were provided by the
manufacturer and have a safety factor of 5% added. Performance test data provided for Jenbacher J620
demonstrate actual emission rates significantly lower than the emission rate used for the purposes of tacking
CO emissions. As a result, the proposed CO emission factors are considered conservative.

Potential emission from the temporary engines (Source Codes: TEMPGEN1 through TEMPGEN5) are
based on the two months (April and May 2026) operation. Operation of the temporary engines will not
overlap with the operation of the main generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33). For the purpose of
potential emissions for the year 2026, emissions were calculated from the operation of the temporary
engines for two months and the normal operation of the facility for the remaining seven months of the year.
All criteria pollutants are below the major source threshold.

Regulatory Applicability

40 CFR 60 Subpart Dc – Standards of Performance for Small Industrial-Commercial-Institutional Steam
Generating Units

Not applicable.

This subpart applies to steam generating units which construction, modification, or reconstruction is
commenced after June 9, 1989 and that has a maximum design heat input capacity of 100 MMBtu/hr or less
but greater than 10 MMBtu/hr.

The fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35 and EU36) are steam generating units with maximum heat input
capacity less than 10 MMBtu/hr and thus are not subject to this subpart.

40 CFR 60 Subpart JJJJ – Standards of Performance for Stationary Spark Ignition Internal Combustion
Engines

Applicable to the generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33) and the black start engine (Source Code:
EU34).

This subpart applies to spark ignition (SI) internal combustion engines (ICE) constructed after June 12, 2006
and manufactured after July 1, 2007.

The generators and the black start engine are SI ICE constructed and manufactured after July 1, 2007, and
thus subject to this subpart. Engines with power greater than 100 HP are subject to emission standards in
Table 1 to Subpart JJJJ.


                                                   Page 4

SIP Application Review                                       VoltaGrid ATL-1 EGU, Application No.29947


The generators are subject to the following:
   • Emission standards in Table 1 to Subpart JJJJ of Part 60, per 40 CFR 60.4233(e)
   • Demonstrate compliance through the purchase of a certified engine or purchase of a non-certified
       engine with a maintenance plan and performance testing, per 40 CFR 60.4243(b)
   • Maintain records in 40 CFR 60.4245(a)

The black start engine is subject to the following:
   • Emission standards in Table 1 to Subpart JJJJ of Part 60, per 40 CFR 60.4233(e)
   • Installation of a non-resettable hour meter, per 40 CFR 60.4237(a)
   • Demonstrate compliance through the purchase of a certified engine or purchase of a non-certified
       engine with a maintenance plan and performance testing, per 40 CFR 60.4243(b)
   • Requirements to be an emergency stationary ICE under this subpart, per 40 CFR 60.4243(d)
   • Maintain records in 40 CFR 60.4245(a) – (b)
   • Submit annual report if used for the purposes of 40 CFR 60.4243(d)(3)(i), per 40 CFR 60.4245(e)

40 CFR 63 Subpart ZZZZ – National Emissions Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants for Stationary
Reciprocating Internal Combustion Engines

Applicable to the generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33) and the black start engine (Source Code:
EU34).

This subpart applies to stationary reciprocating internal combustion engines (RICE) located at major and
area sources of HAP emissions.

The generators and the black start engine are RICE located at an area source of HAP emissions, and thus
subject to this subpart. They are considered new because they will commence construction at an area source
after June 12, 2006. New stationary RICE at an area source meet the requirements of this subpart by meeting
the requirements of 40 CFR 60 Subpart JJJJ. The generators and the black start engines will meet the
requirements of 40 CFR 60 Subpart JJJJ, thus no further requirements apply to these engines.

40 CFR 63 Subpart DDDDD – National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants for Major
Sources: Industrial, Commercial, and Institutional Boilers and Process Heaters

Not applicable.

This subpart applies to boilers and process heaters located at major sources of HAP emissions.

The facility is not a major source of HAP emissions, and thus the fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35 and
EU36) are not subject to this subpart.


                                                 Page 5

SIP Application Review                                           VoltaGrid ATL-1 EGU, Application No.29947


40 CFR 63 Subpart JJJJJJ - National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants for Industrial,
Commercial, and Institutional Boilers Area Sources

Avoidance.

This subpart applies to boilers located at an area source of HAP emissions. Gas-fired boilers are not subject
to this subpart.

The fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35 and EU36) are boilers located at an area source of HAP
emissions; however, they will only fire natural gas, meet the definition of a gas-fired boiler as stated below,
and thus are not subject to this subpart.

“(any boiler that burns gaseous fuels not combined with any solid fuels and burns liquid fuel only during
periods of gas curtailment, gas supply interruption, startups, or for periodic testing maintenance, or
operator training on liquid fuel)”

391-3-1-.02(2)(b) – Visible Emissions

Applicable to the generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33) and the black start engine (Source Code:
EU34).

This rule applies to emission units that are subject to other emission limitation under the Georgia Rules. The
generators and the black start engine are subject to Rules (g) and (mmm) and thus subject to this rule.

The fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35 and EU36) are subject to stricter limitation in Rule (d) and are
not subject to this rule.

391-3-1-.02(2)(d) – Fuel-Burning Equipment

Applicable to the fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35 and EU36).

This rule applies to fuel-burning equipment as defined in 391-3-1-.01(cc).

The fuel gas heaters are fuel-burning equipment and thus are subject to this rule. They are subject to a fly
ash and/or particulate matter emission standard defined by the equation P = 0.5 pounds per million Btu heat
input, and to a visible emissions standard of not equal to greater than twenty (20) percent, except for one
six-minute period per hour of not more than twenty-seven (27) percent opacity.

391-3-1-.02(2)(g) – Sulfur Dioxide

Applicable to the generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33), the black start engine (Source Code:
EU34), and the fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35 and EU36).

This rule applies to fuel-burning sources. The generators, the black start engines, and the fuel gas heaters
will fire natural gas and thus are subject to this rule. They are subject to a sulfur fuel content standard of no
more than 3 percent sulfur, by weight.


                                                    Page 6

SIP Application Review                                          VoltaGrid ATL-1 EGU, Application No.29947


391-3-1-.02(2)(tt) – VOC Emissions from Major Sources

Not applicable.

This rule requires RACT to sources of VOC emissions greater than the threshold for a listed county.

The facility will be located in Newton County, one of the listed counties with a threshold of 100 tpy. The
facility has potential emissions of VOC emissions less than 100 tpy and thus is not subject to this rule.

391-3-1-.02(2)(yy) –Emissions of Nitrogen Oxides from Major Sources

Not applicable.

This rule requires RACT to sources of NOX emissions greater than the threshold for a listed county.

The facility will be located in Newton County, one of the listed counties with a threshold of 100 tpy. The
facility has potential emissions of NOX emissions less than 100 tpy and thus is not subject to this subpart.

391-3-1-.02(2)(lll) – NOX Emissions from Fuel-Burning Equipment

Not applicable.

This rule applies to fuel-burning equipment with a maximum heat input capacity equal to or greater than 10
MMBtu/hr and less than or equal to 250 MMBtu/hr, located in a listed county.

The fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35 and EU36) have heat input capacities less than 10 MMBtu/hr
and thus are not subject to this rule.

391-3-1-.02(2)(mmm) – NOX Emissions from Stationary Gas Turbines and Stationary Engines used to
Generate Electricity

Applicable to the generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33) and the black start engine (Source Code:
EU34).

This rule applies to stationary engines used to generate electricity located in a listed county. The generators
and the black start engine are used to generate electricity and located in Newton County, one of the listed
counties, and thus are subject to this rule.

The generators are non-emergency engines and subject to the emission standard of (mmm)1 during the
period of May 1 through September 30. A performance test will be required to demonstrate compliance, as
set by PTM 2.120.2. Monitoring of the catalyst inlet temperature, pressure drop across the catalyst, and the
reagent injection rate of the SCR of each generator is required by PTM 2.120.3(a) – (c). An annual test
measurement to demonstrate compliance prior to ozone season is also required by PTM 2.120.3(d).

The black start engine will be an emergency standby stationary engine, as defined in (mmm)4, and will not
be subject to the emission standard.

Not applicable.

individual fuel gas heater has potential NOX emissions less than 1 tpy and thus are not subject to this rule.

Permit Conditions

Condition 2.1 limits emissions of CO to 99.0 tpy as part of Title V Avoidance.

Condition 2.2 limits emissions of formaldehyde from each generator (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33)
to 0.022 lb/hr, as part of Avoidance of Air Toxics and Avoidance of 40 CFR 63.2.

Condition 2.3 limits the hours of operation of the black start engine (Source Code: EU34) to 100 hours, as
part of Avoidance of Air Toxics.

Condition 2.4 establishes the applicability of 40 CFR 60 Subpart A and 40 CFR 60 Subpart JJJJ to the
generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33) and the black start engine (Source Code: EU34).

Condition 2.5 sets the emission standards applicable to the generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33)
and the black start engine (Source Code: EU34), per 40 CFR 60 Subpart JJJJ.

Condition 2.6 sets the criteria for the black start engine (Source Code: EU34) to be considered an emergency
generator in 40 CFR 60 Subpart JJJJ.

Condition 2.7 establishes the applicability of 40 CFR 63 Subpart A and 40 CFR 63 Subpart ZZZZ to the
generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33) and the black start engine (Source Code: EU34).

Condition 2.8 requires the fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35 and EU36) to only fire natural gas to be
considered gas-fired boilers for 40 CFR 63 Subpart JJJJJJ Avoidance.

Condition 2.9 sets a visible emission standard to the generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33) and the
black start engine (Source Code: EU34), per Georgia Rule (b).

Condition 2.10 sets a fly ash and/or PM emissions standard to the fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35
and EU36), per Georgia Rule (d).

Condition 2.11 sets a visible emissions standard to the fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35 and EU36),
per Georgia Rule (d).

Condition 2.12 sets a sulfur fuel content limit on all fuel fired in the generators (Source Codes: EU1 through
EU33), the black start engine (Source Code: EU34), and the fuel gas heaters (Source Code: EU35 and
EU36), per Georgia Rule (g).

Condition 2.13 sets a NOX emissions standard to the generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33), per
Georgia Rule (mmm).

Condition 2.14 sets the criteria for the black start engine (Source Code: EU34) to be considered an
emergency generator under Georgia Rule (mmm).

Condition 4.1 requires the operation of the SCR and oxidation catalyst (Source Codes: CD1 through CD33)
whenever the generators (Source Codes: EU1 through EU33) are in operation, as part of Title V Avoidance.

Condition 5.1a requires a non-resettable hour meter on the black start engine (Source Codes: EU33) as part
of Title V Avoidance and 40 CFR 60 Subpart JJJJ.

through EU33) and of the fuel gas heaters (Source Codes: EU35 and EU36), as part of Title V Avoidance.

per PTM 2.120.3(a) for Georgia Rule (mmm).

Condition 5.3 requires monitoring of the pressure drop across the SCR (Source Codes: CD1 through CD33),
per PTM 2.120.3(b) for Georgia Rule (mmm).

Condition 5.4 requires monitoring of the reagent injection rate of the SCR (Source Codes: CD1 through

EU33) after the initial performance test by performing a test measurement, per PTM 2.120.3(d) for Georgia
Rule (mmm).

Condition 6.2 requires an initial performance test for formaldehyde on the generators (Source Codes: EU1
through EU33) to demonstrate compliance with Condition 2.2.

the black start engine (Source Code: EU34) if the requirements of Condition 7.4 are not met, per 40 CFR
60 Subpart JJJJ.

5.1a, per 40 CFR 60 Subpart JJJJ.

(Source Code: EU34) using the record of Condition 7.7 to demonstrate compliance with Condition 7.4, per
40 CFR 60 Subpart JJJJ.

Toxic Impact Assessment

The facility conducted a toxic impact assessment in which the pollutants in the table below were identified
as TAPs of interest because the potential emissions of these pollutants were greater than their respective
MERs. Potential emissions for formaldehyde are based on the proposed formaldehyde limit.

              Pollutant                      Potential emissions            Minimum Emission Rate (MER)
                 --                               (lb/year)                          (lb/year)
           1,3-Butadiene                             119                                 7
           Acetaldehyde                             3,737                              1,107
              Acrolein                              2,298                               85
             Ammonia                               72,707                             24,333
              Benzene                                197                                32
        Ethylene Dibromide                            20                                 4
           Formaldehyde                            11,554                               22

The facility conducted further analysis using SCREEN3 modeling software (version 13043), which
evaluates the maximum 1-hour average impact. Three models were run, one for each emission unit at the
facility: one J620 engine with a unit emission rate of 1 g/s, one black start emergency engine with a unit
emission rate of 1 g/s, and one fuel gas heater with a unit emission rate of 1 g/s.

The J620 engine and the fuel gas heater are to operate continuously throughout the year, so the short-term
and long-term emission rates are identical. The black start emergency engine will only operate 100 hours
per year. The short-term emission rate for this engine is the emission rate found in AP-42 for the TAPs. The
long-term emission rate was determined to be the ratio of 100 hours to 8,760 hours.

The table below presents the result of this analysis. The short-term and long-term impacts for all TAPs were
determined to be equal to or below their allowable ambident concentration (AAC). No further analysis was
conducted.

                            Short-term (15-min)                           Long-term (annual)

                                                  Page 11

SIP Application Review                                         VoltaGrid ATL-1 EGU, Application No.29947

In the case of formaldehyde, the facility used an emission rate of 0.022 lb/hr, derived from performance
test data conducted on six (6) Jenbacher J620 engines of similar size in October 2025 and a safety factor of
20%. Because this emission rate was used to model formaldehyde emissions and because the long-term
impact is just under AAC, the facility will be subject to a formaldehyde limit of 0.022 lb/hr, as required in
Condition 2.2, and will verify this emission rate by conducting a one-time performance test on each
generator, as required in Condition 6.2.

The impact modeling was conducted by limiting the black-start engine to 100 hours of operation per year.
As a result, the black start engine is limited to 100 hours of operation, as required in Condition 2.3.

Summary & Recommendations

I recommend that Air Quality Permit No. 7374-217-0078-E-01-0 be issued to VoltaGrid ATL-1 EGU for
the construction and operation of greenfield power generation facility consisting of 33 natural gas-fired
RICE, two natural gas-fired fuel gas heaters, and one black start emergency generator. The facility will be
a synthetic minor with respect to Title V by limiting CO emissions to 99.0 tpy. The facility will be required
to limit emissions of formaldehyde. Its Public Advisory period ended on December 19, 2025, and comments
were received by the Division.
